# Part 2. Google Drive docs (new)

## D1. Lead FLO Marketing — Morning Copy Review — 2026-10-09 (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1UVvRkdjd2wX7VzgXb1f3vAQgiHmZ6P3zAydJPeo8U7I/edit
- **Last modified:** 2026-10-09
- **Author:** scale@dulcy.io account (Dulcy). Written as an AI-assisted copy review for Alexander. Version LF-20261009-v2.
- **Status in source:** "PRIVATE / PROPOSED / NOT APPROVED FOR USE". Sibling of export `118-DulcyCampaignCopyMorningReview2026-10-08.md` (that one is Dulcy's own review; this Lead FLO one was not exported).
- **Client:** Lead FLO Marketing (client contact Patrick LeMaster). CLIENT-SPECIFIC.

**Summary.** Review copy for three Lead FLO verticals (Tree services/arborists nationwide, HVAC nationwide, Event Rentals) built on two offer contracts: PAGE-1 (free page draft + editable text + recorded walkthrough, publishing separate) and LEADS-5 (five free rental inquiries). Each has Step 1A/1B openings, Step 2 and Step 3 in-thread. Then exact fulfillment contracts, evidence/claim limits, variable rules and a bounded A/B test plan.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "Choose one opening per recipient. Steps 2 and 3 are genuine in-thread replies with no new subject. No spintax or signature is included."
- "A missing first name removes the entire first-name greeting; it never becomes a guessed name."
- "Removed the unsupported “12 companies” claim. No lead, job, revenue, ranking or conversion guarantee."
- "Removed any implication that a meeting can substitute for the promised asset."
- "Do not describe general landscaping businesses as arborists"
- "Do not invent testimonials, insurance, certifications, prices, savings or differentiators. If usable photos or experience evidence are unavailable, do not use the corresponding opening until scope/copy is adjusted."
- "A booking, screen-share or “ready” label does not complete this promise."
- "Do not silently add the historical paid PPL setup fee or prospect-funded ads to the word “free.”"
- "If a call is genuinely required, revise the first-touch offer to disclose it and obtain approval before use."
- "Keep existing accepted prospect promises separate: this new review does not retroactively rewrite what earlier recipients were offered."
- "COMPANY: actual clean brand, required for every version. FIRST_NAME: optional and person-verified; omit the whole greeting for a generic address or missing name."
- "SERVICE_CATEGORY: ... Hold unknown service category."
- "Plain-text synthetic checks do not establish EmailBison rendering or end-to-end QA."
- Test rule: "test opening A versus B within the same approved offer and vertical, with non-overlapping companies and the same send conditions. Do not compare nationwide free pages with regional free leads as a clean copy-only A/B test. Define batch size from Patrick’s real production capacity, not available email inventory."
- Success metric: "qualified businesses that receive the promised item and progress to a relevant paid-service discussion per unique company contacted ... Review after the complete three-step sequence plus 14 days ... Sparse results remain inconclusive. Stop new offers if delivery commitments cannot be met."

**Frameworks.** Offer contract per campaign (paid service behind the sample; what is promised; method and rights; necessary inputs; owner; delivery evidence; approval). Two-person approval: client approves scope/terms/copy; Alexander approves campaign/cohort/final use. Step structure: 1A offer-led opening vs 1B context-led opening -> Step 2 narrows to one service and asks "Which service would you choose?" -> Step 3 adds the walkthrough + "no call or purchase required".

**Example emails (verbatim). Label: Lead FLO, PROPOSED review copy (not approved). Quality: good structure per Dulcy's own review rules.**

Tree services / arborists, nationwide (Offer contract PAGE-1)
Subject options: a page for {SERVICE_CATEGORY} / {COMPANY} estimate requests

Step 1A, recommended offer-led opening:
> {FIRST_NAME},
> I’d like to make {COMPANY} a free page draft for {SERVICE_CATEGORY}.
> It would explain what the work includes, show your relevant experience and give customers a clear way to request an estimate.
> I’d email the page preview and editable text for you to keep. Publishing it is separate.
> Would you like one?

Step 1B, alternative opening:
> {FIRST_NAME},
> When customers compare quotes for {SERVICE_CATEGORY}, it helps to show what they’re getting.
> I’d like to make {COMPANY} a free page draft that explains the work and shows your relevant experience.
> I’d email the preview and editable text for you to keep. Publishing it is separate.
> Would that be useful?

Step 2:
> The draft would focus on one service you want more inquiries about. I’d use your actual service details, relevant work and an estimate-request section.
> Which service would you choose?

Step 3:
> You’d also get a short recorded walkthrough of the page. The preview and text would be yours to keep, with no call or purchase required.
> Want me to make one for {COMPANY}?

HVAC, nationwide (PAGE-1)
Audience: "Established US HVAC installation, repair and maintenance operators. Owners, GMs or relevant senior acquisition decision-makers. No manufacturers, suppliers, software, directories, non-buying corporate HQ or side businesses."
Subject options: a page for your HVAC services / {COMPANY} estimate requests

Step 1A:
> {FIRST_NAME},
> I’d like to make {COMPANY} a free page draft for an HVAC service you want more inquiries about.
> It would explain what’s included, show your relevant experience and give customers a clear way to request an estimate.
> I’d email the preview and editable text for you to keep. Publishing it is separate.
> Would you like one?

Step 1B:
> {FIRST_NAME},
> When customers compare HVAC quotes, it helps to show what they’re getting.
> I’d like to make {COMPANY} a free page draft that explains the service and shows your relevant experience.
> I’d email the preview and editable text for you to keep. Publishing it is separate.
> Would that be useful?

Step 2:
> We’d choose one HVAC service you want more inquiries for and build the draft around it, using your actual service details and customer proof where available.
> Which service would you choose?

Step 3:
> You’d also get a short recorded walkthrough of the page. The preview and text would be yours to keep, with no call or purchase required.
> Want me to make one for {COMPANY}?

Event Rentals top-up, five-lead control (LEADS-5)
Audience: "Established, full-time actual event-rental operators; exclude part-timers, side businesses, planners without rental fulfillment and directories."
Subject options: five rental inquiries / {COMPANY} rentals

Step 1A:
> {FIRST_NAME},
> I’d like to offer {COMPANY} five free rental inquiries.
> We’d first check that we cover your delivery area and the rentals you offer. If it’s a match, I’d email the five inquiries for your team to follow up, with no purchase required.
> Want to check coverage?

Step 1B:
> {FIRST_NAME},
> If {COMPANY} has room for more rental bookings, I’d like to offer five free inquiries.
> We’d check coverage for your rentals and delivery area first, then email the inquiries for your team to follow up. No purchase required.
> Want to check coverage?

Step 2:
> The five inquiries would be for the rentals you offer in the area you serve. Your team would handle the quotes and bookings.
> Which rentals would you like more inquiries for?

Step 3:
> We run advertising for event-rental businesses. These five free inquiries would let you try what we generate before deciding whether to discuss ongoing marketing.
> Want to check whether we cover {COMPANY}’s delivery area?

Event Rentals, national page alternative (PAGE-1; "never an extra step or duplicate send to the five-lead cohort")
Subject options: a page for your rentals / {COMPANY} rental inquiries

Step 1A:
> {FIRST_NAME},
> I’d like to make {COMPANY} a free page draft for the rentals you want more inquiries about.
> It would show what’s included, relevant event photos and a clear way to request a quote.
> I’d email the preview and editable text for you to keep. Publishing it is separate.
> Would you like one?

Step 1B:
> {FIRST_NAME},
> When customers compare rental quotes, it helps to show what’s included.
> I’d like to make {COMPANY} a free page draft for one rental category, using your real service details and event photos.
> I’d email the preview and editable text for you to keep. Publishing it is separate.
> Would that be useful?

Step 2:
> The draft would focus on one rental category, including what customers get, the delivery or setup you actually offer, and a quote-request section.
> Which rentals would you choose?

Step 3:
> You’d also get a short recorded walkthrough of the page. The preview and text would be yours to keep, with no call or purchase required.
> Want me to make one for {COMPANY}?

Conditional insert (verbatim rule): "If Patrick restricts Event to Southern California, insert “in eligible Southern California service areas” after “five free rental inquiries” in Step 1A".

**Bad/rejected (from this doc).** Old Event copy with the "12 companies" claim: rejected as unsupported. Old "leftover-jobs/reactivation approach": "rejected" by Alexander on Oct 7.

**Client facts (Lead FLO only, never reuse):** see the client-facts list at the end of this file.


## D2. MJJ Campaign Scripts — October 28, 2026 (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1HOfhZNeK5yKCirIPpBuJlhf_6-B7TTNhunfrpsRVvw4/edit
- **Last modified:** 2026-10-28
- **Author:** scale@dulcy.io (Dulcy, AI-assisted review draft); client feedback from Ike at MJJ Brilliant.
- **Status in source:** "Private review only; no external writes, sends or launch." Final tightening pass after client feedback.
- **Client:** MJJ Brilliant. CLIENT-SPECIFIC.

**Summary.** Two MJJ Brilliant campaigns (Wholesale repairs; Manufacturing), each with two Email 1 variants, Emails 2-3 in-thread, and an optional Email 4 the client asked for. Each follow-up gives "a different practical reason to respond". Ends with an unsent client note and "Preserved boundaries".

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "Statement-style closes follow the client's direction; question-ending test is a recommendation in the separate note, not silently imposed."
- "each follow-up now gives a different practical reason to respond"
- "My recommendation is to A/B test your statement-style close against a specific response question, rather than assume either will perform better."
- "I’d keep the company history secondary to the buyer benefit—it adds context, but the practical value is more immediately relevant to a cold reader."
- "A fourth email isn’t my default recommendation, but I’ve provided one for each campaign as requested, with useful context rather than a breakup line."
- "No free repairs, price-beating claim, fake-breakup language, false prior relationship or invented completed asset."
- "Repair timing is scoped to agreed jobs; actual scope, timing start point, parts/shipping exceptions and capacity must be confirmed before an unconditional promise."
- "No-MOQ options, 0% duty and 2–3-week timing remain product/order-dependent; no universal customs or delivery guarantee."
- "Two distinct offers, two first-touch variants each, three shared follow-ups each. Fourth step remains proposed, not enabled."
- "This is the strongest review draft from the available facts and preferences, not a guarantee of performance. Stop cosmetic revision; obtain approval and test under the managed workflow."

**Framework.** Email 1 = question hook + concrete differentiators (price/turnaround or MOQ/duty/lead time) with qualifiers + company background line + close. Follow-ups each offer a smaller, concrete next action: choose one category / check one job before turning it away / compare one outsourced category; for manufacturing: one held-off style / required delivery date / compare one style.

**Example emails (verbatim). Label: MJJ Brilliant, review draft after client feedback (good per Dulcy's latest pass; awaiting client approval).**

Wholesale repairs — Email 1 Variant 1. Subject: extra repair capacity
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> Have a repair job your usual bench can’t take on?
> We can handle your overflow repairs at wholesale pricing with a turnaround time of 10 business days on agreed jobs, so you can keep repairs moving without the expense of hiring another jeweler.
> MJJ Brilliant has been a family-owned and operated jewelry manufacturer since 1982.
> You focus on your customers. We'll handle the repairs.

(Note in source: "Client repair Variant 1 is preserved except the disclosed timing qualifier “on agreed jobs.”" i.e. this variant is the client's own copy.)

Wholesale repairs — Email 1 Variant 2. Subject: extra repair capacity
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> Are repairs taking time away from your jeweler’s custom work?
> As your wholesale repair partner, we can handle those jobs at wholesale pricing with a turnaround time of 10 business days on agreed jobs, so your bench can focus on the work you want to keep in-house.
> MJJ Brilliant has been a family-owned and operated jewelry manufacturer since 1982.
> You keep your bench focused. We’ll handle the overflow.

Email 2 — choose what stays in-house (same thread; no new subject)
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> You wouldn’t need to move all your repair work.
> Keep the jobs you want in-house and use us as your wholesale repair partner for the categories that take up more of your bench’s time.
> Send over the repair category you’d like help with, and we’ll confirm what we can handle.

Email 3 — assess a job before turning it away (same thread)
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> If a repair comes in that your usual bench can’t take on, you can check with us before turning it away.
> We’ll review the job details and confirm whether we can handle it, along with the price and turnaround, before you ship anything.
> Send over one job your bench couldn’t take on.

Email 4 — evaluate an existing outsourced category (same thread; optional, client-requested)
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> For repairs you already outsource, you can compare another option without changing your whole setup.
> We can review a typical job and confirm our wholesale price and turnaround, so you have something concrete to compare with your current arrangement.
> Send over the details of a repair you regularly send out.

Manufacturing — Email 1 Variant 1. Subject: 0% duty production
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> Have a jewelry style you’d like to produce without a large first order?
> We offer no-MOQ made-to-order options, 0% duty on qualifying USMCA production and 2–3-week delivery lead times, depending on the piece and order.
> MJJ Brilliant is a family-owned and operated fine jewelry manufacturer since 1982.
> Send over the style and quantity you have in mind, and we’ll confirm the available production options.

Manufacturing — Email 1 Variant 2. Subject: No MOQs made-to-order
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> Are you ordering extra pieces just to meet your manufacturer’s minimum?
> We offer no-MOQ made-to-order options, 0% duty on qualifying USMCA production and 2–3-week delivery lead times, depending on the piece and order.
> MJJ Brilliant is a family-owned and operated fine jewelry manufacturer since 1982.
> Send over the style and quantity you actually need, and we’ll confirm the available production options.

Email 2 — avoid tying up money in extra stock (same thread)
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> A smaller order can leave less money tied up in extra stock and more available for your next style.
> Our made-to-order options let us look at the quantity you expect to sell, rather than start with a large minimum. We’d confirm what’s possible for that piece before you commit.
> Send over a style you’ve held off ordering because of the minimum.

Email 3 — communication and required delivery date (same thread)
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> Our team checks in with production and keeps you updated as your order moves along, so you’re not the one chasing the factory for its status.
> If you have a launch or restock date in mind, we can check the production timing against it before you decide.
> Send over the style and the date you need it delivered.

Email 4 — compare one style before a wider supplier decision (optional, client-requested)
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> You can evaluate another manufacturer without moving your whole collection.
> Start with one style. We’ll review its specifications and quantity so you can compare the proposed price and timing before deciding whether to place an order.
> Send over the specifications for a style you already produce.

Unsent client note (verbatim):
> Ike, I’ve revised both campaigns around your feedback, using your repair email as the base for Variant 2 and the follow-ups. The freebies and price-beating claim are removed, the manufacturing differentiators and company background are included, and each follow-up now gives a different practical reason to respond. My recommendation is to A/B test your statement-style close against a specific response question, rather than assume either will perform better. I’d keep the company history secondary to the buyer benefit—it adds context, but the practical value is more immediately relevant to a cold reader. A fourth email isn’t my default recommendation, but I’ve provided one for each campaign as requested, with useful context rather than a breakup line. Please review the wording and confirm your approval before we move toward launch.

**Bad/removed (from this doc):** earlier drafts with freebies (free repairs) and a price-beating claim — removed at the client's request; fake-breakup lines — banned.


## D3. Email Follow-Up Templates | Ike / MJJ Brilliant (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1LrvwK7V37h4Ytv5_FKPYEDLg2zquMqyJTwTfSCp_Y9I/edit
- **Last modified:** 2026-11-03
- **Author:** scale@dulcy.io (Dulcy), compiled for Ike at MJJ Brilliant from Dulcy's Airtable "Templates" table.
- **Scope note:** this is reply-stage copy (Sean's area), not cold first touches. Included because it is outbound copy and sets the rule for what follow-up copy is allowed to claim.

**Summary.** Dulcy's original generic Airtable reply templates: four responses to interested leads ("I'm Interested", "Let's Book a Call", "Send Me a Video", "What's The Price?") and nine "Meeting Request Ghosted" follow-ups, each with a usage note on when the wording is true.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "The numbered follow-ups are reference options, not an instruction to send all nine."
- "Interested leads: once someone replies positively, continue the conversation manually. Answer what they actually asked, refer to the specific reason they were interested, and then offer a useful next step. Don’t paste the campaign’s next cold email into an active conversation."
- "No response to initial outreach: this is a separate stage. Use the approved campaign follow-up sequence for people who have never replied; the meeting-request templates below assume prior engagement."
- "If an interested lead goes quiet, Alexander’s guidance is to follow up about every three days. Stop or adjust when the person replies, opts out, or asks for a different time. Confirm fit before repeatedly asking for a meeting."
- "Only use claims that are true: a previous call must have happened, a time must actually be held, a video must exist, and pricing or commitments must reflect MJJ’s approved terms."
- On the price template: "Reference only: this is Dulcy’s original pricing template, not MJJ pricing."

**Templates (verbatim). Label: Dulcy generic Airtable templates (good, generic; facts must be made true before use).**

"I'm Interested"
> Hey [Name],
> {{Excitement here}}! {{Brief explanation on the offer}}
> For more information, here’s a quick overview: {{insert value proposition}}.
> If you’re available, I’d love to {{insert offer}}. I’m free on {{insert date}} at {{insert time}} ({{timezone}}).

“Let’s Book a Call”
> Hey [Name], sounds good!
> Does {{insert date}} at {{insert time}} ({{timezone}}) work for you?
> Looking forward to speaking with you!

Send Me a Video
> Hey [Name],
> I just recorded a quick video going over it. It was a bit short however I am more than happy to showcase this to you over a quick call.
> Here’s the video {{highlighting our process/explaining how it works}}: {{Loom or video link}}.
> If you have 10-15 minutes sometime this week, I can go more into detail on how {{offer}}. Let me know what works for you!

What's The Price? (Dulcy's own pricing template)
> Hey [Name], our pricing depends on a few factors, but here’s a general idea:
> We typically have a monthly investment, which takes care of our entire service with no minimum commitment. We work with you to find what you want to achieve and get you the best results.
> //OR
> Our {{service/offer}} is custom-built and tailored to each of our clients based on their needs. A general structure would be {{structure here}}.
> Let’s set up a brief 10-15 minute conversation to go over specifics. I can send over an invite, or if now isn’t the right time, no worries!

Follow up #1 - Meeting Request Ghosted (only if a call really happened)
> Hey [NAME],
> I wanted to follow up on our call—we discussed OFFER, but I didn’t hear back. Does {{specific time}} work for you?
> Let me know if that works, and we’ll lock it in!

Follow up #2 (only if the time is genuinely reserved)
> Hey [Name], I’ve reserved a few minutes for us on {{3-4 days out}} to chat about {{insert offer}}.
> If that doesn’t work, feel free to let me know. Just wanted to get some time on the calendar since you expressed interest earlier.
> Looking forward to hearing from you!

Follow up #3
> Hi [Name],
> I want to make sure we don’t miss the chance to connect about {{insert offer}}. How about we get in for {{specific time}}? If that doesn’t work, let me know a time that does, and I’ll make it happen!

Follow up #4
> Hey [Name],
> I know things can get busy, but I wanted to check if you’re still interested in chatting about {{insert offer}}. I’d hate for you to miss out on how we could [insert benefit/outcome].
> Does {{specific time}} work for you? If not, let me know what does!

Follow up #5
> Hey [Name],
> I want to make sure I’m respecting your time—does {{specific time}} work for our call? Let me know, and I’ll send over the invite.

Follow up #6 (only if actually holding that time)
> Hi [Name],
> I know schedules can get hectic, but I just wanted to remind you of how {{insert offer}} can help you [solve pain point or achieve goal]. I’m still holding {{specific time}} for us—let’s get this on the calendar so we can discuss how we can make this work for {{COMPANY}}!

Follow up #7 (ask before placing an invite)
> Hi [Name],
> I completely understand if scheduling has been a challenge. How about I send over a calendar invite for {{specific time}}? If that doesn’t work, just reply with a time that’s better, and I’ll take care of the rest.
> Looking forward to chatting!

Follow up #8
> Hi [Name],
> I noticed we weren’t able to finalize a time for our call. If now isn’t the right time, no worries—I’m happy to circle back later. Let me know if you’d still like to chat about {{insert offer}}!

Follow up #9
> Hey [NAME], were you no longer interested in having us {{offer}}? Let me know either way—I’d be happy to help if the timing is better later.


## D4. Lead Flo Marketing — Cold Outreach Deliverables (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1D-463f76uWS0w46MmMH3AB8pNTWJ4B0EjM5X8YwZ7os/edit
- **Last modified:** 2026-07-17
- **Author / owner:** scale@dulcy.io (Dulcy; "Alexander / Dulcy Deliverables"). Prepared for Patrick LeMaster.
- **Client:** Lead FLO Marketing. CLIENT-SPECIFIC.

**Summary.** Onboarding deliverable: nine offer-validation concepts for cold outreach (each with hook, offer, best fit, cold CTA, test notes), a story-based case-study template, an onboarding recap, a 5-minute pre-call video script and launch guardrails. It predates D1 (Oct 2026), where the direction moved to PAGE-1 / LEADS-5.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "Validate which cold-traffic offer creates the most qualified interest before scaling campaigns. The offers below are test concepts, not approved pricing or public guarantees."
- Offer 1 note: "Lead with exclusivity and fit, not ad management."
- Offer 2 note: "This is a specific diagnostic, not a vague “free audit.”"
- Offer 3 note: "Do not present forecast ranges as guarantees. Use concrete local observations."
- Offer 5 note: "This is safer than promising revenue or closed jobs."
- Offer 6 note: "Do not use this where seasonality, budget, licensing, or geography makes timing unreliable."
- Case study: "Only include metrics that can be supported by CRM, ad-platform, call-tracking, or client records."
- "Cold copy may reference verified proof, but industry ranges are positioning context—not guarantees."
- "Booked appointments and deposit collection are future capabilities, not current promises."
- "The initial campaign is an offer-validation test. Scale only after the winning offer, vertical, list source, and qualification rules are supported by real response and pipeline data."
- Tree-service story: "The revenue claim should not be used publicly until Patrick verifies the source."

**Framework.** Offer card = Hook / Offer / Best fit / Deliverable / Cold CTA / Test notes. Case-study story arc: Owner and starting point -> What was going wrong -> Why the usual fix failed -> The plan -> Turning point -> Results (only verifiable metrics) -> Client quote -> Closing bridge.

**Cold CTAs (verbatim; Lead FLO; proposed test concepts, not approved).**
- Offer 1 Exclusive Lead Pilot. Hook: "Stop competing with four other contractors for the same homeowner." CTA: “Open to seeing what an exclusive-lead pilot would look like in your market?”
- Offer 2 Shared-Lead Waste Review. Hook: "Find out how much shared leads are actually costing after duplicates, bad fits, and price-shopping." CTA: “Worth running the numbers on your last 20–30 leads?”
- Offer 3 Local Demand Map. Hook: "See where qualified demand exists before committing to a campaign." CTA: “Want me to map the first ZIP codes and service lines I’d test?”
- Offer 4 Qualified-Lead Criteria Builder. Hook: "Define a good lead before paying for one." CTA: “Want the qualification sheet we use to define a billable lead?”
- Offer 5 Replacement-Credit Guarantee. Hook: "If a lead fails the written criteria, it should not count." CTA: “Would a written lead standard plus replacement credits make a pilot worth considering?”
- Offer 6 First-Opportunity Speed Guarantee. Hook: "See a tangible result quickly without locking into a long engagement." CTA: “If the first milestone were written into the pilot, would you be open to testing it?”
- Offer 7 Lead-Wallet Starter Sprint. Hook: "Start with a controlled budget instead of another open-ended retainer." CTA: “Would a capped starter wallet be easier to evaluate than another monthly retainer?”
- Offer 8 Dormant-Lead Recovery Sprint. Hook: "Find revenue in leads the company already paid for before buying more." CTA: “Have 500+ old inquiries or estimates sitting in your CRM? We can show you what is still recoverable.” (Note: D1 records Alexander later rejected the leftover-jobs/reactivation approach for Lead FLO.)
- Offer 9 Closed-Job Economics Plan. Hook: "Work backward from profitable jobs, not vanity lead counts." CTA: “Want me to calculate what a qualified opportunity can be worth before we discuss volume?”
- Recommended initial test: "Start with Offers 1, 2, 4, 5, 7, and 9 across tightly defined vertical lists."
- Core message: "Lead Flo helps local service businesses replace shared, low-control lead sources with exclusive opportunities screened against written criteria. The initial decision is a controlled pilot, not an open-ended agency commitment."

**Case-study template (verbatim headline pattern).** "How [COMPANY / BUSINESS TYPE] went from [RELATABLE BEFORE STATE] to [SPECIFIC BUSINESS RESULT] in [TIMEFRAME]". Closing bridge: "[COMPANY] did not need “more leads.” It needed [EXCLUSIVE / BETTER-FIT / BETTER-FOLLOWED-UP] opportunities tied to a clear definition. If [PROSPECT TYPE] is dealing with [SAME BEFORE STATE], Lead Flo can map a controlled pilot for [MARKET]."

**Case-study interview questions (verbatim).** What was happening before you hired Lead Flo? / What had you already tried, and why did it disappoint you? / What was the breaking point that made you act? / What did you worry might happen if you tried marketing again? / How did Lead Flo define a good opportunity for you? / What changed first? / What result mattered most financially? / What changed operationally or personally? / What would you tell another contractor who has been burned by shared leads? / Which numbers can you verify, and where are they recorded?

**Pre-call video script.** Sales/booking asset (Cole/Sean area), summarised only: Opening, The Problem We Solve (shared inquiries from Angi/Thumbtack/HomeAdvisor), How Lead Flo Works, What We Will Review (work backward from job value, margin, close rate), What to Bring (services and area, job value, last 20-30 leads, monthly spend), Proof and Expectations (tree-service example, framed as "a case study, not a promise"), Close. 4-6 minutes, Loom/Wistia, in confirmation email and 24-hour reminder.


## D5. Active Campaigns Sales Copy Export.txt (Drive text file)

- **Link:** https://drive.google.com/file/d/1ecR5pbdbx0OB777VAmvY7q_8ynPfiGgZ/view
- **Last modified:** 2026-10-13
- **Author / owner:** ike@quickjewelryrepairs.com (Ike Danielson, MJJ Brilliant business development). Shared into Dulcy's Drive. **This is the client's own copy from Instantly, not Dulcy-written or Dulcy-approved copy.**
- **Client:** MJJ Brilliant (and its sister brand Quick Jewelry Repairs; also an "MVEye RJO Member Retailers" campaign and a "Haig Canada" campaign, all signed by Ike with MJJ's Long Island City address). CLIENT-SPECIFIC.

**Summary.** Export of 5 campaigns then live in MJJ's Instantly: EU Luxury (tariff angle, 5 steps), LA Visit Care Plans (3 steps), RJO member retailers repair overflow (3 steps), QJR pawn-shop repair outreach (2 steps), Canada tariff review (5 steps). Useful as the client's baseline/voice and as the "before" to D2.

**Rules.** None stated (raw export). Delays recorded per step.

**Label.** Client-written baseline copy, live in Instantly Oct 2026. Not reviewed by Dulcy in this file. Quality notes against Dulcy rules: long (EU and Canada step 1 are well over 50 words), hard 15-minute-call CTAs, breakup emails ("Should I close the loop on this?") that D2 says Dulcy does not use ("fake-breakup language"). Several factual claims (4M pieces/year, 700+ master jewelers, RJC member, 7-business-day turnaround, 50% Canadian tariff from Aug 19, 15% EU ceiling) come from the client and need verification before Dulcy reuses them. Note the turnaround conflict: this file says "7-business-day turnaround"; D2 (Dulcy, later) uses "10 business days on agreed jobs". Use D2 unless Ike confirms otherwise.

**Examples (verbatim, signatures shortened to "[Ike signature]").**

1. Campaign `MJJ EU Luxury 07Sep26 - Ike`
Step 1 (0 days). Subject: A 15% tariff on every US shipment now — even the small ones
> Hi {{firstName}},
> Since July 2026, EU-origin jewelry lands in the US under a flat 15% ceiling — no exemptions, nothing below it. And since the $800 de minimis exemption was permanently suspended this June, every shipment now needs formal customs entry, regardless of value.
> That means slower delivery, added compliance overhead, and margin erosion on every single US order.
> MJJ Brilliant is a US-owned and operated manufacturer (since 1982) with production in Mexico under USMCA — giving our partners 0% duty entry into the US, without sacrificing quality.
> We produce over 4 million pieces a year, with a 0-MOQ, made-to-order model built for brands that need speed and flexibility, not warehouses full of inventory.
> For a maison already selling into the US, nearshoring part of your production, repairs, or fulfillment through us could mean cutting tariff exposure and delivery time in one move.
> Worth a 15-minute call to see where this fits into your North American operations?
> Best regards,
> Ike Danielson / Business Development | MJJ Brilliant Inc. / 30-30 Northern Blvd, Suite 500 / Long Island City, NY 11101

Step 2 (3 days, in thread)
> Hi {{firstName}},
> Following up on my last note — beyond the tariff exposure, there’s the distance problem. Every piece made in Europe and shipped to a US customer or retail partner adds transit time, insurance costs, and customs friction that a Mexico- or US-based operation simply doesn’t have.
> With our facilities positioned to serve the US market directly, we help brands like yours cut that lag substantially — faster replenishment for wholesale accounts, and faster delivery for direct-to-consumer orders.
> Our dropshipping and nationwide distribution network means your US inventory can sit closer to your customers, ready to move the moment an order comes in.
> For teams managing supply chain or import-export today, this usually translates into fewer expedited-freight fire drills and a more predictable landed cost per piece.
> Happy to walk through what that could look like for your current US volume — do you have 15 minutes this week or next?
> [Ike signature]

Step 3 (4 days, in thread)
> Hi {{firstName}},
> Quick question: today, when a US customer or retail partner needs a repair, where does that piece go?
> For most European maisons we talk to, it ships back across the Atlantic — adding weeks of turnaround, shipping risk, and cost on a service that’s supposed to build loyalty, not test it.
> MJJ Brilliant offers wholesale repair services with fast turnaround and wholesale pricing, done entirely within the US — no transatlantic shipping, no customs delay on returns.
> If your brand sells directly to consumers online, our ReShyne platform takes this further: it’s a white-label, plug-and-play tool that lets your US customers submit and track self-initiated repair requests right from your own site, integrated with Shopify or Magento.
> It runs under your brand, quietly in the background, while we handle the repair itself.
> Would it be useful to see how this could plug into your current after-sales process?
> [Ike signature]

Step 4 (5 days, in thread)
> Hi {{firstName}},
> Stepping back from the details — the common thread in tariffs, shipping delays, and repair turnaround is the same: distance from your end customer. Every part of the chain that sits in Europe adds cost and time once a piece is destined for the US market.
> We built MJJ Brilliant to remove exactly that friction for jewelry brands already operating in North America:
> • 0% duty manufacturing out of Mexico under USMCA, with US ownership and oversight since 1982
> • 0-MOQ, made-to-order production — no minimums, no dead stock
> • Wholesale repairs with fast turnaround, kept entirely on this side of the Atlantic
> • Nationwide distribution, dropshipping, and fulfillment to shorten delivery to your US customers
> • ReShyne, our white-label repair platform, for brands selling direct to consumers online
> None of this requires moving your entire operation — most partners start with one piece of this (often production or repairs) and expand from there once the margin impact is clear.
> If it’s useful, I can put together a quick breakdown of what nearshoring even a portion of your US-bound volume could save on tariffs and turnaround. Would that be worth a look?
> [Ike signature]

Step 5 (7 days). Subject: Should I close the loop on this?
> Hi {{firstName}},
> I’ve reached out a few times about how MJJ Brilliant could help offset the tariff and logistics costs on your US operations — production, repairs, distribution, or ReShyne for direct-to-consumer repairs.
> I know timing isn’t always right, so I’ll leave it here for now. If the tariff situation (or a slow repair pipeline, or fulfillment delays) becomes more pressing down the line, feel free to reach back out — happy to pick this back up whenever it’s useful.
> Wishing you continued success in the US market.
> [Ike signature]

2. Campaign `MJJ LA Visit Care Plans Nov26 - Ike`
Step 1 (0 days). Subject: Jewelry Care Plans: Turn aftercare into a new revenue stream
> Hi {{firstName}},
> Our President, Jason Yakubovich, is in LA the first week of November meeting a few brands.
> We just launched 3-year jewelry care plans you sell at checkout under your own brand. MJJ handles service, insured logistics, repairs and QA; RESHYNE runs enrollment and reporting. You keep 50% of every active plan.

4. Campaign `QJR Pawn Outreach — Active 20/day (Oct 2026)` (brand: Quick Jewelry Repairs)
Step 1 (4 days). Subject: Jewelry waiting on repair?

> Quick Jewelry Repairs / 30-30 Northern Blvd, Suite 500 / Long Island City, NY 11101

> Quick Jewelry Repairs / 30-30 Northern Blvd, Suite 500 / Long Island City, NY 11101
> Prefer no follow-up? Reply “no thanks” and we’ll stop.

(This QJR pair is the closest of the five to Dulcy's rules: short, one concrete low-friction ask, opt-out line.)

5. Campaign `Haig Canada Aug 19 Tariff Review - Ike`
Step 1 (2 days). Subject: Eliminating the 50% U.S. tariff for your jewellery business
> Hi {{firstName}},
> Starting August 19, Canadian-origin jewelry entering the U.S. is set to face an additional 50% tariff, even when qualifying under USMCA.
> If U.S. sales matter to your business, this isn’t a minor customs note. It’s a margin decision—and MJJ Brilliant offers an immediate solution.
> We operate a fully USMCA-compliant manufacturing facility in Mexico alongside our New York headquarters.
> By shifting your production to our nearshore pipeline, your goods enter the U.S. entirely duty-free (0% tariff), bypassing Canadian penalties altogether.
> We also offer 0 MOQ and nationwide fulfillment/distribution, should you need support on that end.
> Worth a 15-minute review before the storm sets in?
> [Ike signature]

Step 2 (2 days)
> Hi {{firstName}},
> The headline is 50%. The right answer is SKU-level.
> For each style, the variables are HTS classification, origin, annual U.S. volume, total landed cost, and what production actually occurs in North America.
> MJJ has no MOQs, so this can begin with one controlled pilot—not a wholesale factory move.
> Want me to send the five-SKU comparison worksheet?
> [Ike signature]

Step 3 (3 days)
> Hi {{firstName}},
> We published a concise breakdown of the tariff action, product-level rules, and why nearshoring is suddenly a boardroom issue:
> https://mjjbrilliant.com/new-u-s-tariffs-reshape-jewelry-manufacturing-as-nearshoring-gains-ground/
> The key takeaway: the cheapest factory quote can become the most expensive route once duty, freight, lead time, and inventory are included.
> Reply “compare” and I’ll send what we need to model one U.S.-bound style.
> [Ike signature]

Step 4 (4 days)
> Hi {{firstName}},
> A second manufacturing route is useless if it cannot execute.
> MJJ has operated since 1982, produces 4M pieces annually, and works with 700+ master jewelers across CAD/CAM, casting, setting, finishing, fulfillment, and distribution.
> We offer no-MOQ and made-to-order pilots and are an active RJC member—responsible sourcing is part of the operating system, not a slide-deck claim.
> We can prove the route on one SKU before you move volume. Open to a 15-minute review next week?
> [Ike signature]

Step 5 (1 day)
> Hi {{firstName}},
> I’ll close the loop after this.
> If your U.S.-bound assortment is not in the covered tariff categories, no action is needed.
> If even one core SKU is exposed, waiting until August 19 means testing alternatives after the margin hit. A one-SKU pilot gives you facts before you move volume.
> Should I close this out, or keep a tariff and production review open?
> [Ike signature]


## D6. Copy of Proposed Human Outbound Email Flows (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1dZ0fQN8m1mOq7IBjPuHtIWeKDAbmoNChsygk-Ay1ijc/edit
- **Last modified:** 2026-11-04
- **Author / owner:** joel@human.marketing (Human Marketing; the client's copy of a proposal; drafting origin UNKNOWN, likely Dulcy-drafted and shared with the client).
- **Client:** Human Marketing ("Human"). CLIENT-SPECIFIC.

**Summary.** Two 3-email flows for Human Marketing's ecommerce prospects: a "Creative" flow (free deep analysis + 4 new designed Meta ads, 48h turnaround) and an "AI search" flow (check how the brand appears in ChatGPT answers to shopping questions + draft one product-page update, free first project). Emails 2 and 3 reply in the original thread and narrow to one product.

**Rules / structure (exact quotes).**
- "Reply in the original thread" (Emails 2 and 3 of both flows)
- AI search Email 3: "Your team would review the copy before anything changes on the site."
- Pattern: Email 2 explains how the free work would be done; Email 3 narrows scope to one product and asks which.

**Examples (verbatim). Label: Human Marketing, proposed flows (status UNKNOWN: not marked approved).**

Creative email flow
Email 1. subject: Four ads to test
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> Let’s get more revenue from your meta ads.
> At no cost, we can provide {BRAND} with
>   - A deep analysis of what should improve on your current ads
>   - 4 new fully designed ads along our creative reasoning for you to test out
>   - 48 hour turnaround
> When you get a bigger ROI from these new ads, we would love to explore if we can continue supplementing your current ad creatives.
> Want us to put this together and send to you?
> [alt] You can test them with your current team. If you like the work, we’d love to discuss helping with ongoing creative.

Email 2 (reply in the original thread)
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> For the creative review, we’d look at the message in each ad and what gives someone a reason to believe it.
> For example, an ad explaining a product’s benefit and one showing it in use can test two different ways of making the same point. That gives your team something more useful to learn from than a headline change alone.
> Would an outside perspective like that help with your next round of creative?

Email 3 (reply in the original thread)
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> We could focus the review and four ads on a product you’re putting more support behind, rather than trying to cover the whole catalog.
> Is there a product your team wants new creative for right now?

AI search email flow
Email 1. Recommended subject: Is ChatGPT recommending {COMPANY}? / Alternate subject: Your products in ChatGPT
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> I wanted to offer your team a practical starting point for AI search.
> At Human, we work with ecommerce brands on search and content. We’d check how {COMPANY} appears in ChatGPT answers to a handful of relevant shopping questions, then draft an update to one product page to better address those questions.
> You’d get the search findings and finished copy together, at no cost for this first project.
> Would that be useful for your team?

Email 2 (reply in the original thread)
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> For the page, we’d look at the questions someone asks when deciding whether a product is right for them: who it suits, how it compares, and what makes it different.
> We’d use your product information to make those answers clearer and show you the reasoning behind the changes.
> Is there a product page you’d want us to start with?

Email 3 (reply in the original thread)
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> The first step would be one product page and a short summary of what we found in the search checks. Your team would review the copy before anything changes on the site.
> Would you like us to put that together for {COMPANY}?

Note: the Creative Email 1 line "When you get a bigger ROI from these new ads" is an implied outcome promise; under Dulcy's no-promise rules it should be checked with the client before use.


## D42. Untitled document — "Las Vegas Market Follow-Up — 3 Cold Email Sequences" (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1J8XRtM49weJKhk6MQqyOQVf9_Eo_6WDXb4U3zGqi7J0/edit
- **Last modified:** 2026-08-07
- **Author / owner:** scale@dulcy.io (drafting origin UNKNOWN; sender in copy is Zack Keahey, Head of Growth, Human).
- **Client:** Human Marketing ("Human"). CLIENT-SPECIFIC. Approval status UNKNOWN.

**Summary.** Three 3-email sequences for retail/wholesale brand owners and buyers met at Las Vegas Market (July 2026): Direct & Warm, Insight-Led, Peer/Trend-Led. Offer: free audit (Profit Diagnostic if on Shopify, else no-obligation consult) on what a D2C channel could add. Every email links https://tools.human.marketing/v.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "Each sequence is 3 emails. Pick one approach per contact (don't mix), spaced roughly 4–5 days apart. Fill in bracketed placeholders before sending."
- Sequence 1 "Best for: contacts you had a real conversation with, or who played the slot machine at the booth."
- Sequence 2 "Best for: contacts you didn't get much face time with, or booth walk-bys who took a card but didn't say much."
- Sequence 3 "Best for: contacts in categories where you know competitors have already gone direct-to-consumer."

**Label vs Dulcy rules.** This is semi-warm (trade-show met) outreach, so "Great meeting you" openers are true only for people actually met. Against Dulcy cold rules: a link in every email (D21 "no links"), "Following up on my note from last week" (D7 lazy follow-up), "last note from me" / "closing the loop" breakups (D25). Good parts: one offer per sequence, plain language, the retail-vs-D2C "who bought" insight.

**Examples (verbatim).**

Sequence 1: Direct & Warm
Email 1 — Day 0/1. Subject: Great meeting you at Las Vegas Market
> Hi [First Name],
> Good to connect at Las Vegas Market last week — hope the rest of the show was good to you.
> I mentioned we work with wholesale and retail brands like [Company] to build out a direct-to-consumer channel: your own site, your own customer file, and a real growth engine beyond wholesale. A lot of the brands we talk to are strong in retail but have never had a clean picture of what a direct channel could add.
> If you're curious, we'll run a free audit for you, either a Profit Diagnostic if you're already on Shopify, or a no-obligation consult if you're not, mapping out exactly what D2C could unlock for your business:
> [https://tools.human.marketing/v]
> No pressure either way. Happy to just chat if it's easier.
> Best, Zack
Email 2 — Day 5. Subject: the number most retail brands haven't seen
> Hi [First Name],
> Following up on my note from last week.
> Here's the thing most retail-first brands haven't sized up: retail tells you what sold. D2C tells you who bought, how often, and what they're actually worth to you over time. That's the difference between selling a product and owning a customer.
> We'll run that picture for your business for free, no strings:
> [https://tools.human.marketing/v]
> Worth 15 minutes to see what it looks like for [Company]?
> Best, Zack
Email 3 — Day 10. Subject: last note from me
> Hi [First Name],
> I'll keep this short; I know inboxes get crowded after a trade show.
> If expanding into D2C is even a maybe for [Company] this year, the offer's still open, free audit or free consult, no commitment:
> [https://tools.human.marketing/v]
> If the timing's not right, no worries at all, just let me know and I'll leave it there.
> Best, Zack

Sequence 2: Insight-Led
Email 1 — Day 0/1. Subject: Do you know your customer, or just your buyer?
> Hi [First Name],
> We met briefly at Las Vegas Market, so I'll get straight to the point.
> Most wholesale and retail brands know their sell-through. Very few know their actual customer, who's buying, how often, what they're worth over time. That data lives with the retailer, not with you.
> A direct-to-consumer channel changes that: you own the customer file, the repeat revenue, and the real margin picture, not just the wholesale number.
> We'll show you what that could look like for [Company], free:
> [https://tools.human.marketing/v]
> Best, Zack
Email 2 — Day 5. Subject: retail vs. D2C margins (the real comparison)
> Hi [First Name],
> One more thought on this. Wholesale margins are usually fixed and known. D2C margins are variable, but they're yours to improve, and most brands have never actually run the numbers to compare the two side by side.
> That's exactly what we do in the free audit: a real Profit Diagnostic if you're already running a Shopify store, or a no-cost consult mapping out what D2C could add if you're not:
> [https://tools.human.marketing/v]
> Happy to walk you through it whenever's useful.
> Best, Zack
Email 3 — Day 10. Subject: closing the loop
> Hi [First Name],
> Last note on this one. If a direct channel is on your radar for [Company] at all this year, the free audit offer is still on the table, no pitch, just the numbers:
> [https://tools.human.marketing/v]
> If it's not the right time, that's completely fine, feel free to reach out whenever it is.
> Best, Zack

Sequence 3: Peer / Trend-Led
Email 1 — Day 0/1. Subject: why more wholesale brands are opening a direct channel
> Hi [First Name],
> Good to connect at Las Vegas Market. One thing that came up a lot in booth conversations: more wholesale-first brands are standing up their own D2C channel, not to replace retail, but to own the customer relationship retail can't give them, and to build a second, more profitable growth line alongside it.
> If that's something you've thought about for [Company], we'll run a free audit showing exactly what it could unlock:
> [https://tools.human.marketing/v]
> Best, Zack
Email 2 — Day 5. Subject: what a direct channel actually adds
> Hi [First Name],
> Following up; wanted to be specific about what "going direct" actually adds beyond another sales channel:
> - You own the customer file instead of renting it from a retailer
> - You get real cohort data: who buys, how often, what they're worth
> - You see your actual margin, not just what wholesale pricing allows
> We map all of this out in a free audit or consult, tailored to whether you're already on Shopify or starting from scratch:
> [https://tools.human.marketing/v]
> Best, Zack
Email 3 — Day 10. Subject: still here if useful
> Hi [First Name],
> Last one from me. If exploring a direct channel for [Company] is something you'd want to look at this year, happy to run the free audit whenever works:
> [https://tools.human.marketing/v]
> Otherwise, great meeting you at the show, best of luck with the rest of the year.
> Best, Zack

## D7. Twenty West Media - Instantly Audit (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1OMkuuTM4oAHQT_vwGYuy_R4PkS9RMPz_FGrxaf7BJbE/edit
- **Last modified:** 2026-04-13
- **Author / owner:** scale@dulcy.io (Dulcy; written in Alex's voice: "At Dulcy, we use private IP infrastructure"). Companion files: "Twenty West Media - Instantly Audit raw" (same day, draft of the same audit; read via search snippet only, treated as a duplicate) and the sheet "Twenty West Media Instantly Audit Files" (data, not copy).
- **Client:** Twenty West Media (TWM). CLIENT-SPECIFIC. The audit reviews TWM's own pre-Dulcy Instantly campaigns (sender "Ryan L", TwentyWest).

**Summary.** Dulcy's audit of TWM's existing Instantly setup: infrastructure, deliverability, three campaign "buckets" (Free Marketing Strategy, Free audit, Website Landing Page) with line-by-line copy critique, reply-handling critique, then recommendations (infra, ESP, masking, bounce failsafe, campaign structure, lead sourcing, copy method) and a 20-step TLDR. The copy critique is the most explicit "bad copy" teaching in Drive.

**Copy rules (exact quotes).**
- Subject line: "Try not to come off as a “marketing email” aim to make the subject line look like an internal email"
- "Never say quick follow up // just bumping this up. Stands out as youre marketing them"
- "The multiple choice answer is phased out (not as effective anymore compared to 3-4 years ago)"
- "“Just let me know” or none action taking CTAs statistically lead to worse reply rates then asking for the user to take an action"
- On scarcity lines: "reads as the marketing tactic such as the countdown or “limited seats available” which most people think now “yeah right”"
- "No Signature (who are you?)"
- "Legally technically you need an opt out doesn't need to be a link keep it natural"
- "A/B testing can’t be 4 separate things (subject + entire copy) no one to find a definitive winner since there are so many variables"
- "A/B Testing not conclusive unless offer validating"
- "No one wants a marketing strategy (Not valuable)" / "No one truly deep down wants to improve rankings" / "No one wants a short audit"
- "15-minute call is a very high ask for a low consciousness prospect on the problem + solution. You need an easier CTA"
- "If you’re open to it is asking them to shoo you away"
- "“I’d love to” - we always say this but prospect get the “ick” from it. Ask yourself “What’s in it for them?”"
- "“We specialize in identifying and fixing those issues to drive consistent lead growth.” and so do 100k agencies ... how can we turn this into a “I noticed {issue} - solving {issue} leads to {benefit} which gives you {benefit of benefit} can I show you how to solve it?"
- "IF it is local businesses ... they have no idea what any of this means: Search visibility - Page structure - Conversion flow"
- "“If it helps” Already implying it might now or hasnt before"
- "Note as your steps go down the CTA should get easier for them to say yes (Ex. Mind if I send a video? Can I send more info? May I send the document? Would you be open to a demo? Are you the right person to speak to about this?)"
- "4 days a bit long for a sequence break"
- "“I haven’t heard back, so I’ll pause outreach for now.” - Similar to the “I know you hate cold emails but let me pitch you anyways”. No way someone would read past this."
- "No value in the email. Its positioned to benefit you rather than the prospect. You must focus everything on why you why now and why them."
- On "Yours is ready on our end, just haven’t sent it yet": "This gives a little bait and switch energy ... a feeling of lying is not a very good first impression. Even if it was true."
- Subject suggestion: "I would have changed it to “our homepage | our lead flow | quick question”" and "saying like quick idea for {company} lead flow is just saying this is a marketing email" / "You always have to think in the shoes of the prospect"
- "Becareful leading with a website offer because it is a ton of trust up front ... hence our conversation on leading with seo / ppc instead and then branching into selling website on the backend. Plus some people take their website very personal so wording is very important."
- TLDR 16: "Keep the Email Copy Under 75 Words" — "Shorter copy is generally performing better right now."
- TLDR 17: "Keep Spam Content Score as Close to 0 as Possible"
- TLDR 18: "Always Use Spintax" — "Every email should include strong spintax variation before launch ... intro phrasing / CTA phrasing / wording choices / subject lines"
- Campaign structure: "separate campaigns by: Industry / Offer ... You should not be sending broad mixed campaigns across very different niches."

**Reply-handling rules (exact quotes; Sean's area).**
- "for the very first positive reply ... Keep it very simple and to the point she’s shown interest just mention hey does Tuesday or Thursday at XYZ time wrk better for you?"
- "There is this old sales technique called alternative advance which is giving option A and option B and asking them to choose."
- "Don’t thank her for getting back to her or thank people for their time as it degrades your time and authority giving them ownership"
- "Same with great questions doesnt need to be in there feels like sales talk"
- On price questions: "He is never going to care about the cost when he doesnt know the value ... move this conversation from the cost to giving him a general idea of how it works and ask a follow up question and try to book after"
- Speed to reply: "Should be aiming for sub 15 minutes."
- "From this there is no question to end the email not leading the prospect to any next steps."

**Copy framework (verbatim, "How We Build Better Copy").**
1. Define the Exact ICP (who the person is, what they care about, what their business looks like, what language they use, what pain they are already experiencing)
2. Find the “Investment Piece” — "something that shows they are already aware of the problem or already trying to solve it. Examples: using blogs, running ads, investing in SEO, posting regularly, updating their website"
3. Show Authority or Ask a Strong Question — "an authority statement or a “poke the bear” question ... make the prospect feel that we understand their business and problem as well as, or better than, they do."
- Example framework: SEO offer to roofers investing in blogs. Instead of “Do you need more leads?” / “Can we help grow your business?”, use: “We noticed this specific issue in what you are already doing” / “This may be the reason you are not getting the result you expected” / “We solve this exact problem — want me to show you how?”
- Cold-traffic offer goal: help someone who has never heard of you understand "what you do / why they need it / why they should trust you".

**Good/bad examples (verbatim).**

BAD (TWM reply, critiqued as too long, thanks prospect, "great questions", 15-min ask):
> Hi Sarfraz,
> Thanks for getting back to me.
> Those are great questions. To give you a brief overview, we focus on improving visibility on high-intent platforms like Google, as well as relevant Social and industry-specific channels where your target audience is active.
> The best way to determine the specific leads you could go after would be to have a quick chat about your ideal customer profile. I can then walk you through the opportunities I've identified for Mighty Capital.
> Would you be open to a brief 15-minute call to discuss this in more detail? You can book a time that works for you here: [Calendly link]
> Best regards, Ryan L / TwentyWest

GOOD (Dulcy rewrite of the same reply):
> Hi Sarfraz,
> Your biggest visibility gains would be through your current site content and branching that to your LinkedIn and Google page. Going after b2b tech companies we want to raise the bar on how these founders and operators make their decision on searching for terms tied to product led growth, seed to series a investors, vc partners and questions around scaling their pmf.
> When they search the exact questions and topics, it will naturally lead them to the kind of investor you are, with your case studies, portfolio credibility, and operator-focused positioning doing more of the selling for you.
> I can show you where you can make some easy improvements. Would you have some time on Tuesday or Thursday at 3 PM your time to go over them?

BAD (TWM price reply; "I appreciate you get a lot of these emails" called out: "what does this even mean lol"):
> That's a fair question. I appreciate you get a lot of these emails.
> The cost can vary depending on your specific goals and the current state of your online presence. To give you an accurate price, I'd need to quickly understand what you're looking to achieve.
> I'd be happy to hop on a brief 15-minute call to discuss your goals, share the gaps I noticed, and provide a clear idea of the investment.
> You can book a time that works for you here: [Calendly link]

BAD (TWM booking reply: too many options):
> Absolutely.
> I have some availability this week - tomorrow Thursday, April 9th, at 2:30 / 3:00 / 4:00 / 4:30 PM UTC OR Friday April 10th, at 2:30 / 3:30 PM UTC
> If neither of those days/times works, please feel free to pick a time that suits you best from my calendar: [Calendly link]

BAD (TWM website-mockup reply: no closing question; expiring link):
> Hi Hans,
> Not a problem.
> Right now, your website has solid potential, but with an updated look, clearer messaging, and stronger call-to-action flow, it can build much more trust and turn more visitors into actual inquiries.
> As mentioned, we put together a quick mock-up to show what that could look like in practice (focused on getting you more leads from the same traffic).
> Here’s the home preview that will last for the next 48 hours: [Replit link]
> Just to note, this is only one example. We can design it in any direction you’d like, based on your vision and goals. We can also generate leads for you from interested potential clients.
> Happy to walk you through it if you have a few minutes.

BAD lines quoted from TWM's campaigns (critiqued): “Few Patterns Stood Out”; “short marketing strategy outline”; “We only put together a handful of custom strategy outlines each week - didn’t want to build one unnecessarily.”; “Noticed a few areas”; “In your space”; “Fix exactly that”; “When these gaps exist, competitors usually absorb that traffic.”; “Cleaner flow, stronger positioning, clearer CTA.”; “Nothing overbuilt, just structured to generate more booked calls.”; “Nothing crazy.. Just higher intent design.”; “We only build a small number of these preview mockups each week. Yours is ready on our end, just haven’t sent it yet. When would be a good day to showcase this?”; “I haven’t heard back, so I’ll pause outreach for now.”

**Benchmarks stated.** "Cold Email Metrics — 20,000 Leads / 4% RR Rate - 800 / 10-15% PR Rate - 80-120 / 30-40% Booking Rate - 24-48". Bounce failsafe: pause at "roughly 4%" (matches Dulcy rule). Volume "10–15 cold emails per day per inbox". Warm-up "1:1 ratio" first 2 weeks then "2:1 warm-up-to-cold". Verification "MillionVerifier with LeadMagic", target "sub-2% bounce rates". These are planning benchmarks, not results.

**TWM campaign results quoted in the audit (TWM's own pre-Dulcy data, client-specific):** Bucket 1 Free Marketing Strategy: 61,717 leads, 17% bounce, 434 replies (0.5%), 5 PRs. Bucket 2 Free audit: 80,896 leads, 26% bounce, 300 replies (0.4%), 13 PRs. Bucket 3 Website Landing Page: 49,504 leads, 11% bounce, 249 replies (0.3%), 7 PRs.


## D8. Twenty West Media GPT Scripts (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/19JQ1piUsq1cx_KRyrzo0BmukdHYZ9zbfryuG8nJMFMk/edit
- **Last modified:** 2026-08-18
- **Author / owner:** scale@dulcy.io (Dulcy).
- **Client:** Twenty West Media. CLIENT-SPECIFIC campaign (AI-search / ChatGPT visibility angle for local businesses; Step 2 mentions "members", so the first target looks like gyms/studios; UNKNOWN which vertical exactly).
- **Status:** UNKNOWN (no approval marker).

**Summary.** A 3-step sequence. Tab 1 is the spintaxed version, Tab 2 the plain read-through. Step 1 = "I asked ChatGPT for {KEYWORD} in {CITY}" competitor-comparison hook + offer to send notes. Step 2 = stat + three ranking factors + soft ask. Step 3 = "not sure if you're the right person" + recorded video offer. P.S. placeholders for a case study and a joke opt-out.

**Rules / patterns (from the doc).**
- Spintax format: `{option A|option B}`; sign-off spun across 10 options; `{SENDER_FULL_NAME}` variable.
- Placeholders still open in the doc: "PS. CASE STUDY", "P.P.S. or tell me to JOKE" (the joke opt-out line is UNKNOWN / not written).
- Note: Step 2's statistic ("Google recently reported that 67% of consumers under 35 ...") is unsourced in the doc. Verify before use (Dulcy rule: never invent proof).

**Example emails (verbatim). Label: Twenty West Media, draft.**

Tab 1 (spintax)
Step 1:
> {I asked GPT for|I asked ChatGPT for} "{KEYWORD} in {CITY}" and the first 2 places that came up were {COMP}.
> Didn't see you guys come up.
> I spent {a few minutes|a little time} digging into why those {KEYWORD} were getting recommended and pulled together {a few notes|some notes} for {COMPANY}.
> {Want me to send them over?|Mind if I send them over?}
> --
> {Best regards|Kind regards|Warm regards|Sincerely|Thank you|Thanks|Yours truly|With best wishes|Best wishes|Warm wishes},
> {SENDER_FULL_NAME}
> PS. CASE STUDY
> P.P.S. or tell me to JOKE

Step 2:
> {So essentially a bit more on what I was referring to previously.|Just a bit more context on what I was referring to previously.|Figured I'd add a little more context to my last email.}
> {Google recently reported that 67% of consumers under 35 actively use AI to research and make decisions about local businesses.|Google recently reported that 67% of consumers under 35 are using AI to research local businesses before making decisions.|According to Google, 67% of consumers under 35 now use AI when researching local businesses.}
> We've found that what impacts {recommendations the most|AI recommendations the most} for {KEYWORD} is:
> - {Having significantly more high-quality reviews than competitors|Having more high-quality reviews than competing {KEYWORD}|Building a stronger review profile than nearby competitors}
> - {Optimizing your Google Business Profile for the exact searches people are making|Making sure your Google Business Profile is aligned with the searches people are actually making|Optimizing your Google Business Profile around the exact terms people are searching}
> - {Building pages around the specific neighborhoods and areas you're trying to attract members from|Creating pages focused on the neighborhoods you want to attract members from|Building location-specific pages around the areas you're trying to reach}
> {I'd be more than happy to show you where you guys stand and what to focus on first. Would you be open to that?|Happy to show you where you currently stand and what I'd focus on first. Open to that?}
> -- [spun sign-off] / {SENDER_FULL_NAME}
> P.S. or tell me to JOKE

Step 3:
> {I wasn’t sure if I should send this to you or someone else.|Not sure if you’re the right person for this, but figured I’d reach out.|Wasn’t sure if this should go to you or someone else.}

> {Mind if I share the video here?|Want me to send it over?|Open to taking a look?}
> -- [spun sign-off] / {SENDER_FULL_NAME}
> P.S. or tell me to JOKE
