## D11. campaign_decision_framework — "When to top off, rebuild, or pivot a campaign" (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1Ij0rWY_bxHePyiW4HPgBurOc5bpNLOAPG9lB5FVjSaY/edit
- **Last modified:** 2026-05-05
- **Author / owner:** scale@dulcy.io (Dulcy). Mostly Atlas's decision (top-up vs new vertical vs new offer vs copy change), but it sets when copy changes are the right lever.

**Summary.** Three-row decision table keyed on a client's email-to-lead ratio vs the agency average.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "Performing at or above average ... the campaign is working and just needs more volume." -> "Top off the campaign. Pull previous targeting, build a lookalike list, enrich, validate, and add net new contacts into the existing campaign."
- "Underperforming by 40%+ ... Can you take the same offer into a new vertical? This is usually the highest-probability play. Same offer, different industry, more relevant messaging. Launch as a fresh campaign." -> "Adapt messaging for the new industry and launch as an entirely new campaign."
- "Underperforming and locked in ... Can the client create a new offer? If yes, build around it. If no, you’re limited to copy iterations and angle testing. This has the lowest likelihood of a meaningful performance change." -> "New offer if possible. If the client won’t budge, iterate on copy and angles within the existing setup."

**Implication for Scribe.** A copy rewrite is the last lever, not the first. When the offer or vertical changes, copy is rewritten for the new industry as a new campaign, not edited in place.

**Examples:** none.


## D12. Make an Offer Cold Outbound Ready (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1g_hCWkL8rsvGOjghxg6Puvkij-5n8_lVUw-HSWe87gk/edit
- **Last modified:** 2026-10-07
- **Author / owner:** scale@dulcy.io (Dulcy; "Our best example (Dulcy)"; reads like Alex's training/content doc). Offer-design doc (Forge, now folded into Scribe), with one email template and one worked example.

**Summary.** Service vs offer; three offer levers (Service Lead Magnet, Guarantee/performance, Fast start); the Hormozi free-offer gym story; offer packaging checklist; demand gen vs demand capture; one cold email template plus a worked example.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "Unless your offer is unique, you are selling a commodity. Your service will not convert on cold traffic. To convert cold traffic you need a strong outcome-driven offer."
- "Cold outbound isn’t the place for “We do email marketing. Interested?”"
- "Service: what you do (mechanism)" / "Offer: what they get + how fast + why it’s safe (outcome + terms)"
- "The framework (3 levers): 1. Give free value (Service Lead Magnet) 2. Guarantee / performance (risk reversal) 3. Make is easy to start (fast start + low friction)"
- "Our best example (Dulcy): “I can build you a free lead list of X accounts in your ICP – want it?”"
- "Rule: relevance > free stuff" / "Your free thing should preview the paid result"
- Deliverable must be: "Fast (minutes, not days) / Repeatable (template-able) / Automatable (AI/code) / High perceived value"
- Hormozi story: "The free offer business got 9X more leads and closed at the exact same rate." / "Offer a free campaign. A free service. A free month trial. Get them in the door. Upsell later."
- "Cold outbound is a volume game" / "If you can get 100 conversations instead of 10, you win even if some are low quality"
- Guarantee patterns: "Speed guarantee: “We deliver the first asset in 48 hours or it’s free.”" / "Outcome guarantee: “If we don’t generate X qualified meetings in 30 days we work for free until we do.”" / "Milestone guarantee: “If you don’t see X by week 2, you can cancel and keep the deliverables.”"
- "“Customer satisfaction guaranteed” = meaningless (everyone says this)" / "“Dedicated CSM” - not a guarantee"
- "Only offer the guarantee to great-fit prospects ... Scope it to a milestone (week 1-2) ... Use “work-for-free-until-X” or “cancel -and-keep-delierables” instead of unlimited refunds"
- "10 closed + 1 refunded beats 5 closed + 0 refunds"
- Fast start line: "We'll do the first tangible thing before you feel locked in.”"
- "What to avoid: Generic “audit” offers (in sophisticated markets, nobody cares)" / "Make it specific (“2-3 fixes”) and fast (“10-minute Loom”), not vague (“full audit”)"
- Offer Packaging Checklist: "ICP ... Outcome ... Deliverables ... Time to value ... Risk reversal ... Proof: 1-2 tight proof points (numbers or examples) ... CTA: one simple next step (reply “yes” / send a link / ask 2 questions)"
- "Demand gen: cold email / cold ads -> you must create awareness + desire from scratch ... That’s why “service pitches” fail on cold"

**Lead-magnet examples (verbatim).** "E-com email agency: redo the welcome flow (1-2 emails) or subject line bank" / "Paid ads agency: 10 ad angles + 5 creative concepts based on their current positioning" / "SDR/appointment setting: 25 target accounts + 2-message opener per account cluster" / "Web/dev: teardown + prioritized fixes with “impact score”" / Loom: "I’ll review your [funnel/channel] and show you the 2-3 biggest leaks + what I’d do first".

**Email template (verbatim).**
> Hey [First Name],
> Saw [specific, scrapeable signal about the company].
> We already have [asset / resource / solution] based on [what that signal suggests they need] and recently helped [similar company / client achieve specific result in timeframe].
> Want me to send over [specific low-friction asset]?
> [risk reduction mechanism]

**Worked example (verbatim; label: good, illustrative; the 31% result is an illustration, not Dulcy proof).**
> Hey Mike,
> Saw your Meta Ad Library has 14 active ads, but only 2 new creatives launched in the last 30 days.
> We already mapped out 6 new angles based on your current winners and recently helped another apparel brand increase new customer revenue 31% in 45 days.
> Want me to send over the creative brief?
> No pitch attached. If the ideas are bad, ignore [text ends here in source]

**Tension to flag.** The guarantee patterns here conflict with the current client-facing rule set (D1, D2: no lead/revenue guarantee unless the client approves exact terms). Use guarantees only when the client has approved them.


## D13. HOW TO WRITE HIGH-CONVERTING COLD EMAIL COPY IN 2026 (Google Doc) + D14 transcript "Today, I'm going to make cold email copywriting ... stupid simple" (Google Doc)

- **Links:** D13 https://docs.google.com/document/d/1HEEyHpXJHrCG2LOoMFMr1q0r-YkvRXL_OAA70b39Xsc/edit (modified 2026-04-02). D14 https://docs.google.com/document/d/16ZGTmawOYm-H9SKogYJt6z8r4I2AuRqaWUej6xPZ0sk/edit (modified 2026-05-12). Also `Cold_Email_Copywriting_2026.docx` (1jYkLTaZ_3CM08y6FoEes23J4lINrmVUr, 2026-04-02), the Word version of D13 made the same day (not re-read; treated as duplicate).
- **Owner:** scale@dulcy.io. **Author of the content: a third-party YouTube creator (agency owner), name UNKNOWN.** D14 is the raw video transcript; D13 is a structured write-up of it. Not Dulcy proof: "$220,000+ in revenue", "200 calls in the first 70 days", "52 interested replies" are the creator's claims.

**Summary.** Two jobs of every cold email: build curiosity (dream outcome) and earn trust (perceived likelihood of achievement; Hormozi value equation). Three dream-outcome types (money, time, status). Three trust types (social proof, third-party authority, knowledge as proof). Two structures (Question -> Proof -> CTA; Offer -> (Proof) -> CTA). Three CTA types. Real campaign breakdowns. Rules of thumb and a pre-send checklist.

**Rules (exact quotes, D13 unless noted).**
- "The Non-Negotiable Formula: 1. BUILD CURIOSITY — make them want what you're offering 2. EARN TRUST — make them believe you can deliver it ... Without BOTH, they will not reply."
- "Curiosity = the Dream Outcome (what you'll do for them) / Trust = the Perceived Likelihood of Achievement (why they should believe you)"
- "Sometimes trust is earned outside the email — prospects Google you. This is why a strong digital footprint (content, social presence) amplifies your outbound results."
- "Status is extremely underrated."
- "The outcome must feel like it's specifically for this person ... Describing who it's for first — 'for people running ads like you,' 'for solo founders like yourselves'"
- "Key rule: Make sure the dream outcome is presented in plain language — not just numbers and stats."
- "The more you can layer the similarity between your proof case and the prospect, the better."
- "make sure the proof reinforces the specific dream outcome you're selling."
- "Critical rule: The authority you cite must be recognized and trusted by YOUR specific recipient." (D14: "You never want to reference some sort of niche authority like Taylor Herren for example in cold email ... to an audience that does not know Taylor Herren.")
- "Aim to include two proof elements per email."
- Structure 1 "Use this when: You're not sure how aware your prospect is of the problem you solve. The question 'pokes the bear'"
- "You can only sell a single solution in a cold email."
- Structure 2 "Use this when: You're confident enough in your offer to lead with it directly. Often the proof is baked directly into the offer itself". (D14: "This is my preference because I think most proof that is like social proof kind of thing isn't doing a great job in the early stages")
- Rules of thumb table: "30–75 words is your target length" / "One CTA only" / "One solution per email" / "Two proof elements per email" / "Proof must match the dream outcome" / "Authority must match the recipient's world" / "Personality and humor differentiate"
- D14: "Most of our emails are most successful are between 30 and 70 words. I don't even think they go above 75 most of the time."
- D14: "A scammer would never go to this level of effort." (on detailed follow-ups)
- Checklist final check: "Could a scammer have sent this? (If yes, add specificity)" / "Does this feel like it was written for THIS person specifically?"

**Frameworks.**
- Dream outcome types: Money / Time / Status (with examples: 'We helped a solo founder like you generate $80K in 90 days.' / 'What if your team never had to chase warm leads again?' / 'What if people just knew who you are — and you got invited to speak at events?')
- Trust types: Social Proof ("I did the thing.") / Third-Party Authority ("They found it.") / Knowledge as Proof ("I know this about you specifically.")
- Structures: Question -> Proof -> CTA; Offer -> (Proof) -> CTA. Optional signal on top ("I saw you posted 10 blogs last month — how are you turning those into Reddit-native content?").
- CTA types: Interest-based ("Would you be interested in..." / "Is this worth a quick chat?"); Resource-based (Loom video, audit, lead magnet, breakdown); Permissionless (deliver value upfront, ask if they want to go deeper).

**Examples (verbatim; third-party creator examples; label good, generic).**
- Same offer, different audiences: Audience A (founder who doesn't post content): "What if your team did content so your business was more acquirable — and you could eventually exit?" Audience B (founder already posting): "We can enhance what you're already doing to turn your existing content into more pipeline — which is probably more believable than starting from scratch."
- Third-party authority: "Google says 25% of their references for product-led search come from LinkedIn."
- Knowledge as proof. Subject: Stole your competitor's customer list (kind of)
  > "What if you could steal customers from your competitors?"
  > "I wrapped up the followers of [Brand Name] into a CSV and found that around 20% are likely ICP fits as [ICP Name]. Want to check it out?"
  > P.S. We use data like this to get outcomes like [X] for [Brand Name].
- Poke the bear: "What are you doing to make sure your three fresh sales hires have engaged leads to work every day?"
- Blog -> Reddit (Structure 1): Question "Are you making sure the great content you have in blogs also becomes Reddit posts that ChatGPT delivers to your priority customers as answers?" Offer + Proof "We take all that content for companies like [Blank] and use agents with humans in the loop to turn blog content into Reddit-native content — so your best content gets distributed everywhere."
- Blog -> Reddit (Structure 2): "If I could turn all 10 of your blogs per month into Reddit-native content that ranks in Google's product-led searches — 40% of which now pull from Reddit — would you be interested in seeing the audit I already did for your business?" CTA variants: "Would you be interested in a 2-minute Loom?" / "Would you be interested in a short discussion about your Reddit strategy?" / "Would you be interested in seeing the breakdown?"
- Campaign 1 (community trips; 2,400 contacts, 52 interested replies, ~26-30 calls): Dream outcome 'We're partnering with community owners to run a trip with their community that pays $10K-$30K.' Friction reducer 'All we do is send three emails to your community to see if there's interest. We're only paid when you are.' Follow-up opener: "I realize this is probably crazy — you probably didn't even believe me. Let me explain better." + how it works + three itinerary ideas + full case study. "Of all interested replies, ~50%+ graduated to calls. The trust gap was the bottleneck — and the follow-up closed it."
- Campaign 2 (founder story / AI search): third-party stats "600 million eyeballs are on [platform]", "Reddit is the #2 source for search". Lesson: "When your offer is ahead of the market's awareness, use third-party authority to validate the CATEGORY before pitching your solution."
- Campaign 3 (video game IP, "ultra short, one of the most successful"): "How do you know you're not sitting on multiplayer gold mines in your single-player catalog?" [2-3 named titles] "We're helping publishers spot these gold mines inside their portfolio. Would it be unreasonable to think we'd spot even just one incredible earning opportunity for you — on the house?"

**Prompts:** none.


## D15. cold_email_template_library_organized (Google Doc, two identical copies)

- **Links:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/19eqsrsWGu4wwOPruniBMWdbgm4CVKv6E8kGqNKhHG-4/edit and duplicate https://docs.google.com/document/d/1AUzXA6Weu5K5hR67kInbRfJ8BQcFNZHzinFelNQh944/edit (same size, created 20 seconds apart)
- **Last modified:** 2026-04-21
- **Author / owner:** scale@dulcy.io (an AI-organized reformat of the Notion Cold Email Playbook, N1, plus N2/N3 and some new templates).

**Summary.** Reorganizes the swipe file into: 00 Frameworks (subject-line types, CTA bank, Top 16 CTAs, P.S. bank, core frameworks, lead-magnet theory, personalization ideas, offer ideas by service, writing rules), 01 Master Templates grouped by campaign type (Desired Outcome, Pain/Problem, Lead Magnet, Video/Loom Ask, Case Study/Social Proof, Interview/Whitepaper, Referral/Right Person, Local/Geographic, Hiring Trigger, Competitor/Comparison, Audit/Research/Observation, Guarantee/Risk Reversal, Follow Ups, General), 02 Offer-Specific Index, 03 Niche Index, 99 Archive Recommendations. Most template text is identical to N1/N2/N3 (see those sections). Only the parts that are new are quoted below.

**Rules (exact quotes, new).**
- "Writing Rules / Training Notes: Keep scripts below a 3rd-grade reading level when possible. / Make scripts as short and simple as possible without losing the core value. / Use 5–6 touchpoints across LinkedIn, SMS, call, and email. / Test multiple angles in Email 1 and scale winners."
- Hard CTA list here drops the "Only for Database Reactivation" label that N1 has.

**99 - Archive Recommendations (verbatim; this is the only place the library labels BAD copy).**
- "Too Generic: 'If we could help generate 1-5 clients…', broad 'We help X get Y in Z time', stand-alone 'Interested?' and 'Worth a chat?'"
- "Too Hype / Risky: extreme guarantees, oversized revenue claims, blanket vertical promises without proof"
- "Duplicate Concepts: repeated 'mind if I send a video?', repeated 'not sure if this is for you', repeated 'we helped case study', repeated 'can I send a doc?'"
- "Needs Rewrite: anything that feels botty, vague, or built around unbelievable guarantees instead of a grounded observation or proof point"

**Templates not in N1/N2/N3 (verbatim; generic swipes, label good unless archive rules above apply).**

High Turnover / Staffing Template. Subject line: {{function}} turnover
> {{first name}} - saw your {{department}} team has grown to {{team size}} people recently.
> High turnover in {{department}} can be brutal - are you finding candidates who actually stick, or still cycling through people?
> Asking because we just helped {{similar company}} reduce their {{department}} turnover by {{percentage}} by finding candidates with {{specific qualification}}.
> Worth sharing what we learned about sourcing people who actually stay?

Ad Creative Gap Template. Subject line: {{company}} ad creative
> {{first name}} - just scrolled through {{Company}}'s ad library.
> Noticed you aren't using {{specific creative format}}. Can I make you a sample one?
> PS - Only asking because we just made one for DTC Brand A, and it was their winning creative over BFCM.

Ad Library Review Sample. Subject line: {{company}} ad creative
> {{first name}} - just scrolled through {{Company}}'s ad library.
> Noticed you aren't using founder-forward video creative with heavy retention editing.
> Can I make you a sample one?
> PS - Only asking because we just made one for DTC Brand A, and it was their winning creative over BFCM.

Page Speed Report Lead-In. Subject line: {{company}} page speed
> {{first name}} - just ran {{Company}}'s website through a speed test.
> Your homepage is loading in {{load_time}} seconds, which typically costs you about {{percentage}} of potential conversions.
> I put together a quick report showing {{number}} specific fixes that could get you down to {{target time}} seconds.
> Want me to send it over?
> PS - Every second of load time costs about {{conversion impact}} in conversions on average.

Free Service Reactivation Example (contains an explicit old-vs-new label)
> Old way: if we could help generate 1 - 5 clients every month with our [unique mechanism], would you be interested in speaking?
> New way: {{name}} - when's the last time you reached out to past customers to bring them back in? I'd love to run a reactivation campaign for you completely free to book up your calendar over the next month.
> Then, after determining they are qualified and can benefit, deliver the free service. After they are satisfied, ask about what else they are doing for marketing and if they want help.
(Label: "Old way" = BAD (too generic); "New way" = GOOD.)

AI Workshop Local Proof / Helping Area Companies with AI. Subject line: helping {{area}} companies with AI
> {{first name}} - we just finished helping the owner of {{company}} (also in {{area}}) win 10 hours back per week with a basic, on-site AI training workshop.
> Does that sound like something you could benefit from?
> Asking because we're on-site with them and a few other {{area}} business owners, and wanted to see if you'd be interested as well.
> Worth a chat?

Nearby City Local Proof / Nearby Area Generic
> {{firstName}} - noticed you're in {{city}}. I live {{in nearby area}}.
> Thought it might be interesting to speak on {{what you do}}.
> We've helped other {{city}} area businesses {{drive results with it}}, so I figured I'd ask.
> Let me know if you're interested.

Nearby Area Local Template (yoga studio example)
> Hi [NAME],
> Noticed you're in the [area of city] area, so I thought I'd reach out.
> We're a yoga studio located nearby at [address].
> For [Company Name]'s next retreat or offsite, would you be interested in a group session?
> Best, [NAME]
> PS - They're great for team bonding. [Past customer], an [area of city] business, just finished their 4th with us!

**Indexes (summary).** 02 Offer-Specific Index maps templates to Lead Generation, PPC/Paid Ads, SEO/CRO/Web, Email Marketing/CRM, Content/Creative/UGC, AI/Automation, Amazon, Hiring/Staffing, PR/Branding, Local Business. 03 Niche Index maps to Agencies, DTC/Ecommerce, SaaS, Law Firms, Real Estate, Home Services, Healthcare, Hospitality, Local Biz, Martech, Amazon. Example: Agencies -> Need more ICP Leads?, Sponsor Clutch, Why Isn't? Case Studies, Straight Lead Magnet, HubSpot Automation Video Pitch.

## D17. The GTM cheat code nobody talks about (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/19sg2866VjiP2bhBI7k3h4MeejvIUN7yyhGXZcOks7jg/edit
- **Last modified:** 2026-05-01
- **Author / owner:** scale@dulcy.io. Content is a newsletter issue (links via kit-mail3.com tracking, i.e. a third-party newsletter; author UNKNOWN). Not Dulcy proof.

**Summary.** Use Reddit to mine the buyer's own words. Three layers of pain; a 5-step language-mining workflow with a "Reddit Fetch" Claude Code skill; build a language bank for subject lines, openers and CTAs.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "Most cold email sounds like it was written by someone who read a case study about your industry once. Vague pain. Generic outcomes. Language that could describe literally any company in your space."
- "Reddit is the place where your buyers go when they stop performing."
- "Layer 1 - Surface pain: “We're not getting enough leads.” ... Layer 2 - Real pain: “We've tried three agencies and our pipeline is still the same.” ... Layer 3 - Existential pain: “I've been doing this for 8 years and I don't know if outbound even works anymore. If I can't figure this out by Q3, I'm probably getting replaced.”"
- "Most GTM copy lives at Layer 1. The best copy mirrors Layer 3."
- "Your copy can't speak to all of them. But it can speak to one of them so precisely that the right ones feel like you read their diary."

**Workflow (verbatim).** "1. Pick 3-5 subreddits where your ICP hangs out 2. Pull top 200 posts filtered by pain keywords (struggling, failing, help, tried everything, nothing works) 3. Feed output into Claude - extract recurring phrases, emotional language, specific failures, and the fear underneath each complaint 4. Build a language bank - real phrases you drop into subject lines, openers, and CTAs 5. Test against existing copy and watch response rate move"

**Tool named:** Reddit Fetch skill, https://github.com/ykdojo/claude-code-tips/blob/main/skills/reddit-fetch/SKILL.md

**Example (verbatim, illustrative):** "I have 40 reps and I genuinely don't know why 30 of them aren't booking meetings. I've done call recordings, I've done training, and nothing works. I'm starting to think it's a hiring problem but I'm terrified to admit that to my board."

## D18. How to Use Spintax (Agency Ignition)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1wUWmnP1W81uKzcFdi5xjbbpj-2gNqCQZJThX5ALzROY/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-12-26
- **Author:** Alex (Agency Ignition).
- **Overlap:** export `129-Backup-Andre-Spintax-Prompt.md` has a similar GPT prompt. The variation-count rule and the signature example below are new.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "Spintax is a method for adding variations to your email content so your messages don’t all look identical. The goal is simple: reduce pattern detection by rotating wording in your subject line, body, and even your signature."
- "Spintax format looks like this: {option 1|option 2|option 3} Every time the email sends, the tool randomly picks one option."
- "The more variations you have, the safer you usually are. If your subject line never changes, it can look suspicious to filters."
- "You want variation, without turning the email into a weird AI mess."
- "My recommendation would be to have anywhere between 1-2x greater than your current total lead pool. Ex. My campaign is targeting 1,000 people on a 3 step sequence. I should have 1,000-2,000 different variations per step bare minimum. Even so, I ideally aim for 500k-1M to be safe."
- "Yes, your signature can be spintaxed too."

**Examples (verbatim; label as in source).**
- BAD subject (no spintax): “Hey {{first_name}}” — Variations: 1
- GOOD subject: “{Hey {{first_name}}|Quick question, {{first_name}}|Got a minute}?” — Variations: 3
- BAD body (no spintax):
> Hey {{first_name}},
> I noticed you’re the director of marketing at {{company_name}} and wanted to see if we could help increase your revenue by 40% in 90 days.
> Would you be open to discussing this further?
> Variations: 1
- GOOD body:
> {Hey|Hi|Hello} {{first_name}},
> {I noticed|It came to my attention} {you’re director of marketing at|you’re handling marketing at} {{company_name}} and {I wanted to see|was curious} if we could {help improve your revenue by|increase your revenue by} 40% in 90 days.
> {Would you be open to discussing|Does this sound like something worth discussing further?|Would you be interested in learning more?|Would you find that useful?|Would this be something we can discuss?}
> Variations: 3 × 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 × 5 = 240
- GOOD signature (Dulcy):
> –
> {Best regards|Kind regards|Sincerely},
> {Alexander Woung|Alex W.|A. Woung}
> {Founder of Dulcy|Founder @ Dulcy|Dulcy|Dulcy Media|Dulcy.io}

**Prompt (verbatim) — "Spintax Prompt for GPT".**
> I'd like to introduce you to the concept of "spintax" (also known as spin syntax), which is a method used to generate unique variations of a text by using placeholders and alternative words or phrases. This is especially useful when creating multiple instances of similar content while still keeping it unique and engaging.
> Spintax uses curly brackets { } to enclose different options for a word or phrase and the pipe symbol | to separate each option. When the text is processed, one option is randomly selected from each set of brackets, creating a unique combination each time.
> Here's an example of a spintax-formatted sentence:
> "{Hello|Hi|Greetings}, {GPT|AI|Language Model}! {How are you|What's up|How's it going} today?"
> When processed, it can produce various outputs like:
> Hello GPT! How are you today? Hi AI! What's up today? Greetings Language Model! How's it going today? I hope this helps you understand spintax better.
> Remember that each choice or words or phrase must be compatible with every other choice so all phrases must be able to be combined randomly not just 1,1,1 2,2,2 but 1,3,2 and 1,2,1 etc. Ensure that all variations make grammatical sense.
> Use no more than 4 examples of spintax in each sentence and update the following text to use spintax:
> {EMAIL COPY}

## D19. Crafting Effective Subject Lines (Agency Ignition, Dec 2025)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1aiUZ8UOB2U4wUYwUvSVNM5Znv1FX_QQQ98_UqkAOARE/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-12-21
- **Author:** Alex (Agency Ignition). (A different, older doc with the same title, 1DJ_flOPVKrdjf4Y2erKTs-FIiKuCLBv6zEIrK0v1Y1I, owner tyler@borks.io, 2024-08-22, is third-party; see skipped list.)

**Summary.** Expanded version of N1's subject-line section with Alex's newer opinions: subject lines matter less; Dulcy does not track opens; the internal-email test; personal swipe notes.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "The best subject lines look like an internal email. Not “marketing.” Not clever. Not try-hard."
- "We also don’t track open rates, so there’s no reason to obsess over this. A data point from a formal poll suggests that only 22% of respondents chose the subject line as the primary reason for opening."
- "This initial step is becoming less important year over year, most people are just scanning and deciding if they want to read. If you overthink it, it starts to look weird, and the prospect gets negative before they even open."
- "So here’s the rule: Make it feel like a coworker emailing another coworker. Short. Normal. Easy to understand in half a second."
- Desired Outcome: "Use these when your offer is end-result focused (more leads, more revenue, more booked calls, more customers)."
- Pain: "Use these when you’re selling a pain reliever ... This works best when the email body starts by calling out the pain and then quickly shows the path out."
- Reference: "Use these when you have a strong data point on the prospect (real context that makes the email feel personal without being corny). ... it can be slower and not always worth the effort versus simpler subject lines."
- Curiosity: "This is the most common used because it creates a small “open loop” in their brain."
- "If your subject line could realistically be sent inside a company Slack/email thread, you’re doing it right."
- "I would send the email to myself before launching a campaign and then come back to it 2-3 hours later when I am super busy and see if it catches my own attention in my own inbox."

**Subject lines (verbatim, with Alex's notes).** Quick question "(not using as much anymore but statistically is one of the best)" / {FIRST_NAME}? / thoughts {FIRST_NAME}? / have you seen this {FIRST_NAME}? / question {FIRST_NAME} / can I get your opinion {FIRST_NAME}? / opinion {FIRST_NAME} / [your service] question / question about [your service] / [your service]? / **our [your service] "(my go to)"** / {RESTAURANT}’s food - yes or no? / {RESTAURANT}? / re: [your service] / [service] / Contact more [ICP]? (new). Type examples: Helping your team with (outcome) / Need more (outcome)? / Looking for (outcome)? / More (outcome)? / Have trouble with (problem)? / Not enough (problem)? / Low reply rates? / Congrats on (accomplishment) / Just saw (case study / post / announcement) / Enjoyed (accomplishment)? / Quick question (first name) / Real quick / Have you seen this (first name)? / Thoughts (first name).

## D20. Real Script Breakdowns (Agency Ignition)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1Qvs5saFc0CiPU46I0tp5RxNMGpI0KVSq8nnoVo8NCjc/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-12-21
- **Author:** Alex (Agency Ignition).

**Summary.** 19 scripts from the swipe file (text identical to N1 templates), each labelled with a campaign type and two annotations: "WHY I BUILT IT THIS WAY" and "WHY IT WORKED". The annotations are new and are Alex's own reasoning. Script 8 is new. All scripts are labelled as working (good). Third-party brand names and numbers in them are swipe examples, not Dulcy proof.

**Annotations (verbatim, by script; script text in N1 unless shown).**
1. Direct Offer ("send my thoughts" soft CTA) = N1 "Positive Personalized Vibes". Built: "Starts with a real observation so it feels like a real email, not a blast." / "Uses “positive → but” to call out pain without sounding like an attack." / "Proof is specific and measurable (CVR + ARPU)." / "CTA is permission-based (easy yes, no meeting asked)." Worked: "It reads like a person who actually looked at the brand." / "The “mind if I send…” CTA lowers resistance and starts a thread." / "Even if they don’t want a call, they’ll often say “sure, send it.”"
2. Case Study Campaign = N1 "Note for NAME LEADS?". Built: "Subject looks like an internal thread." / "CTA is a routing question (super low friction)." / "Case study is used as the reason for relevance, not “look at me.”" Worked: "People reply fast because it’s easier to answer than “want a call?”" / "Even “send it to X” is a win (you get the right person + engagement)."
3. Direct Offer (2-step) = N1 "Quick 1/2 Step". Built: "Step 1 is pain check only (no pitch = more replies)." / "Step 2 uses “reason I ask” to make the follow-up feel natural." / "Loom CTA is micro-commitment." Worked: "Step 1 gets fast “yes/no/not yet” replies." / "Step 2 converts because it’s proof + a simple next step."
4. Education-Based (white paper / interview) = N1 "Interview White Paper". Built: "You’re not “pitching” — you’re positioning as research." / "Social proof is baked in (already interviewed others)." / "The trade is clear: their time → report + link." Worked: "Lower defensiveness than a direct sales email." / "Gets you in the door, then you can pivot naturally into backend."
5. Risk Reversal (guarantee-driven) = N1 "Leverage Case Study Guarantee". Built: "Leads with their win (their case study) to make it feel earned." / "Guarantee flips risk." / "Proof makes the guarantee believable." Worked: "Guarantees force attention in saturated markets." / "The logic is simple: “you already proved this works, let’s amplify it.”"
6. Loom Video Strategy = N1 "Saw you aren’t using USP Video". Built: "Calls out a single gap." / "Doesn’t explain everything in text." / "CTA is simply permission to send." Worked: "“Sure” is easy." / "Once they agree, they’re engaged and you can guide the conversation."
7. Case Study (proof stack + doc CTA) = N1 "Growth Partner Case Study". Built: "Proof stack is operator-style: transformation + logos + close rate." / "Doc CTA = micro yes." Worked: "Reads like a practitioner, not a marketer." / "“Doc I prepared for you” feels custom, even at scale."
8. Lead Magnet Campaign (NEW script, verbatim):
> Hey {FIRST_NAME},
> Could I put together a personalized cold email campaign to leads similar to {CASESTUDY}, to book meetings for {COMPANY}? It’s at no expense to you.
> Since you’re in {CITY}, happy to walk you through it over lunch sometime this week, my treat.
Built: "Front-end offer first (foot in the door)." / "“No expense” removes friction." / "Lunch/local creates trust fast." Worked: "People respond to tangible free work." / "Makes you feel real (not a spammer)."
9. Direct Offer = N1 "Comp 1 vs Comp 2 Google". Built: "Anchors in a real-world buying moment (customers choosing between options)." / "Pain is implied, not screamed." / "CTA is simple and conversational." Worked: "Local owners instantly “get it.”" / "It doesn’t feel like generic marketing language."
10. Direct Offer (local leads) = N1 "Home Improvement Phone Number". Built: "Subject is plain and internal-looking." / "Priority question is direct." / "Follow-up uses a “best number?” CTA (micro yes)." Worked: "Contractors respond to straight language." / "Phone question forces a reply more often than a booking link."
11. Direct Offer (reputation angle) = N1 "Review Call Out". Built: "Personalization is actually relevant (review = revenue + reputation)." / "Compliment creates goodwill before the ask." / "Ends with a low-friction question." Worked: "Owners care about reviews." / "Feels like you actually looked, not scraped LinkedIn."
12. Lead Magnet (pattern interrupt) = N1 "Why Isn’t? Case Studies" (option a). Built: "Pattern interrupt opener." / "Uses their existing proof as leverage." / "Front-end offer is tangible (1K leads)." Worked: "Calls out an obvious missed opportunity." / "Easy yes because it’s free and specific."
13. Lead Magnet = N1 "Free Cold Call Script". Built: "Best-person question is a built-in reply trigger." / "Lead magnet is clear (script)." / "PS is proof without turning the email into a pitch." Worked: "People like “assets” they can use immediately." / "Even if they don’t buy, they reply."
14. Direct Offer = N1 "Product Ranked Higher Amazon". Built: "One sentence. Outcome only." / "Permission CTA lowers resistance." Worked: "Amazon sellers understand ranking = money." / "Great opener when you want a super short email."
15. Lead Magnet = N1 "Free UGC Content". Built: "Calls out a visible pain." / "“Free package” is a tangible front-end offer." / "“Want to hear more?” is a micro yes." Worked: "UGC is easy to understand and test quickly." / "Most brands will at least respond out of curiosity."
16. Lead Magnet (free ideas / workshop) = N1 "PR Agency B2B Tech". Built: "Value first: you’re not asking for trust, you’re earning it." / "The “took liberties” line makes it feel human." / "Proof is heavy and specific." Worked: "This is a real pattern interrupt." / "You’re already doing the work before they even respond."
17. Direct Offer (strategy suggestion + proof) = N1 "PPC for Gyms". Built: "Proof of “I found you naturally” (search + local)." / "Suggests one specific strategy (member stories)." / "Proof is in units they care about (members/month)." Worked: "Feels like a helpful nudge, not a pitch." / "Strong local relevance."
18. Lead Magnet (publication) = N1 "Newsletter Publication". Built: "Front-end offer is distribution (something they actually want)." / "Simple ask." / "PS is social proof." Worked: "It’s a door-opener." / "You can upsell backend after trust is built."
19. Lead Magnet (pure value) = N1 "Straight Lead Magnet". Built: "Straight value, no fluff." / "No complicated steps." / "Easy yes." Worked: "People respond to tangible assets." / "Even if they don’t buy, you start a conversation."


## D21. 3 Simple Rules for Effective Messaging (Agency Ignition)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/10uylrkr3YwirTjDcVL9MjYqnYWg2ZZxNcLVXGG2UEfY/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-12-21
- **Author:** Alex (Agency Ignition).

**Summary.** Three rules: earn the open (preview = subject + first line), keep them reading (short, specific, real), make the reply effortless. Plus DOs and DON'Ts.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "This is not normal copywriting. In cold email, one wrong word can get you ignored, flagged, or pushed to spam."
- "And if you’re coming into this thinking the goal is to “sell”… You already lost. Your goal is to find out if they have a problem you can solve."
- "The best cold emails feel like a normal email someone would actually send."
- Rule 1: "The “open” comes from the preview: your subject line / your first line" ... "Your preview should: match what the email is actually about / never scream “pitch” / sound like it could be from a client, vendor, partner, or someone internal" ... "Questions work because humans can’t help themselves." ... "The point isn’t to be clever. The point is to look normal and get opened."
- Rule 2: "You’re not writing an essay. You’re writing something they can scan in 5 seconds." Structure: "1. call out the problem (in their language) 2. connect it to your solution (simple) 3. one credibility point". "Credibility can be: a relevant case study / a known competitor you work with / media mention / a specific result. Not “we’re experts” that means nothing."
- Rule 3: "If your CTA makes them think, you lose. Your CTA should be: low resistance / one-step / no links / easy to reply to with one word" ... "You’re not trying to close a deal in email #1. You’re trying to get a reply so the conversation starts."
- DOs: "Keep it short (4 sentences max is a good ceiling (50-75 words))" / "Write at a simple 4th grade reading level" / "Match tone to the ICP (cosmetics brand ≠ auto dealer)" / "Use a low-friction CTA" / "Include credibility if it’s real" / "Be specific about the problem + outcome"
- DON'Ts: "Don’t overpromise" / "Don’t be vague about what you do" / "Don’t confuse them with fancy wording" / "Don’t bait and switch" / "Don’t use salesy / promotional language"

**Examples (verbatim; good).**
- Subject: question about [PRODUCT] / Line 1: I came across [PRODUCT] (I think in a google ad?) and checked out your guys' [relevant thing that i might look at given what i do]...
- Gmail preview as seen: "question about sports winter jacket I came across [PRODUCT] (I think in a google ad?) and checked out your guys…."
- Subject: note for {FIRST_NAME} and {COLLEAGUE} / Line 1: {FIRST_NAME} - mind letting me know if you or {COLLEAGUE} is best to send this to?
- Body: “High turnover in sales can be brutal. Are you finding candidates who actually stick, or still cycling through people? Asking because we just helped COMPANY reduce their sales dept. turnover by 11%, by finding candidates with proven track records at similar sizes.”
- CTAs: “Want me to send the strategy?” / “Cool if I share an example?” / “Should I forward the details?”

Note on reading level: D21 says "4th grade"; N2 says "below 3rd grade". Both are Alex's; treat as "3rd-4th grade".

## D22. A/B Testing Your Scripts the Right Way (Agency Ignition)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1Uo98lc2RfVbz2cFWWNKnL4la3drc5moyD-ROVta-jLs/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-12-21
- **Author:** Alex (Agency Ignition).

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "When running cold email campaigns, you have to always be testing."
- "The Golden Rule of A/B Testing: You should only test one variable at a time to understand exactly what is driving the response rate. This might be the subject line, the call-to-action, or even something small like the opening sentence."
- "Testing cold email campaigns should be similar to how you would treat ad campaigns."
- Things to test: "Subject lines / Copy / CTA / Offer / Warm Up/No Warm Up / Amt of Emails Being Sent / Google vs Outlook / Lead Lists / ESPs"

## D23. End Result-Focused Scripts (Agency Ignition)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1kpuR2KMk_t7wth3XwXg8zD4OzOdygMLWpK6O0WiDmi4/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-12-21
- **Author:** Alex (Agency Ignition).

**Summary.** Build the email around the CTA; the CTA ladder (same as N1, including "Hard (Only for Database Reactivation)"); eight script angles with when-to-use, goal and example; cut words; sell the result; easy yes; how not to write.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "When most people write cold emails, they write the email first… then slap a CTA on at the end. That’s backwards. A good cold email is built around the CTA. Every line either: earns attention / builds belief / lowers resistance / makes the “yes” feel easy. If a sentence doesn’t help the CTA, it’s dead weight."
- "Decide what you want in the first email: Reply (most common) / Permission-based reply (“can I send ___?”) / Micro yes (video/example/details) / Booked call (rare in email #1 unless you’re extremely precise with your targeting)"
- "You don’t need 6 angles in one email. Pick one angle and commit."
- Problem Sniffing: "The win here is credibility. If it’s vague, don’t use it."
- Hyper-Relevant Case Study: "Not “we’ve helped companies like you.”"
- Lead Magnet: "something they can actually use (has actual value; something they would pay for.)" / "*Fixing an actual problem (no tangible value unless its targeting a specific problem and the audit magnet is the solution)" / "If it’s not obviously valuable in 2 seconds, it’s not a real lead magnet."
- Direct Offer: "You can’t be vague here. Direct offer needs a direct outcome."
- Lead Magnet V2: "High effort, but it separates you from 99% of inbox noise."
- "If a word or phrase doesn’t push the prospect toward a response, cut it." / "A smart person admires simplicity. A dumb person admires complexity."
- "Sell The Result, Not The Service ... The service is the backend. The result is the hook."
- "Earn the meeting. Don’t demand it."
- "How Not To Write Cold Emails. These kill campaigns: Talking about yourself too much (they don’t care) / Irrelevant personalization (college, hometown, random facts) / Overcomplicated CTA (too big of an ask) / Inbox essays (long = ignored) / Fancy formatting/images (hurts deliverability + screams “marketing”)"

**Framework: 8 script angles (name / use when / goal).** Poke The Bear (prospect sits in a pain but ignores it / make them feel the problem) · Problem Sniffing (real data point: ads running, hiring signals, ratings/reviews, tech stack, funnel issues, competitor movement / "I’m not guessing, I saw something.") · Hyper-Relevant Case Study (same-world result / borrowed trust) · Lead Magnet (skeptical, burned, saturated market / earn trust first) · Opportunity Cost (already doing it inefficiently / show the leak: wasted spend, missed follow-up, slow response time, lost leads, poor conversion points) · Stars Align (stacked timing signals: hiring, expanding, launching, funded) · Direct Offer (validating demand fast / outcome-first) · Lead Magnet V2 (maximum pattern interrupt / value inside the email).

**Examples (verbatim; labels from source).**
- BAD poke-the-bear: “How do you manage your leads?” GOOD: “What’s your approach to making sure your hottest leads aren’t left waiting while juggling everything else?”
- Problem Sniffing: “I noticed {{company_name}}’s product pages are ranking well, but there seems to be a dip in customer reviews over the last quarter. It reminds me of a similar trend we saw with {{competitor}} before we helped them turn things around by tweaking their post-purchase engagement. We increased their review rate by 25%, which led to a nice bump in conversions. Would it be okay if I shared how we fixed it for them?”
- Hyper-Relevant Case Study: “If we helped Company X (a manufacturing firm) save 30% on production costs using advanced supply chain optimization…”
- Lead Magnet: “I was checking out your website and thought of ideas for a video that could boost your sales by showcasing the best of providing delicious coffee and quick bites for everyone. I'd actually be willing to create your first video for free, as I'm confident you'll be impressed by it. Would you be open to discussing these ideas?”
- Opportunity Cost: “I made a short video explaining how "Jack's Apparel" could potentially increase sales by 20% in just two months by expanding to Amazon. Would it be okay if I send you the video to check out at your convenience?"
- Stars Align: “We noticed your team just expanded into new markets and are hiring sales managers. Would you be open to discussing how we can help boost your lead generation efforts with minimal ramp-up time?”
- Direct Offer 1: “Hey {{first_name}}, if I could help you reduce your operational costs by 15% within the next 30 days, would you be open to a quick discussion?” / 2: “Hey {{first_name}}, if I could generate 10 new sales leads for you by next week, would it be worth a chat?”
- Lead Magnet V2: “Hey {{first_name}}, Noticed you guys helped {{prospect_case_study}} in their marketing efforts. Here are 3 contacts that fit the exact criteria of your past clients: {{contacts}} (Jane Doe, CEO, Visual Studios. jane@visualstudios.com, +1 555-333-4444 ) We can put together 1k more for you for free, if that’s something you’re looking to explore - would that be okay?”
- Cut words. BAD: “We specialize in providing a wide range of solutions that are designed to optimize your outreach process in order to increase efficiency.” GOOD: “We help you book more calls with better prospects.”
- Sell the result. BAD: “We manage your cold email system…” GOOD: “We help you consistently book 20+ sales meetings/month through cold email.”
- GOOD CTAs: “Can I send a quick video explaining?” / “Mind if I show you an example?” / “Want me to share the breakdown?” / “Open to me sending details?” BAD CTA (too much, too early): “Do you have 30 minutes this week?”


## D24. Types of Cold Email Campaigns (Agency Ignition)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1PjeL9Mq_wk6SZH94ohySIiH0Cqz88R6DUyJ-AWhvcDc/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-12-29
- **Author:** Alex (Agency Ignition; first person "running my agency").
- **Overlap:** export `089`-`094` (Case Study / Risk Reduction / Education / Loom / Lead Magnet / Direct Offer campaign approaches) cover six of these seven types in shorter form. New here: demand-capture vs demand-generation fit per type, the Market Research type, Dulcy's own examples and Alex's opinions.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "Out of my years writing cold emails and running my agency, I believe the most important thing for results comes down to copy. You can have the best technical setup and the best leads, but if you write a terrible cold email, you won’t get results."
- "the best scripts you’ll ever run are the ones you create yourself. If I say “this is the best script” and thousands of people copy it, it gets watered down fast."
- "Before you pick a campaign type, you need two things: 1. Do you have an offer? ... 2. Are you selling a demand capture or demand generation offer?"
- Lead Magnet: "This is one of my favorites if it’s possible" / "The key is it has to be something you’d normally pay for, and it should open the door to the bigger problem your core offer solves."
- Loom: "This doesn’t work as “can I send you a video audit” by itself. It works when you call something out, then offer the loom as the breakdown/fix"
- Market Research: "The goal is not to book a call. It’s to collect info." / "We rarely run this"
- Education/Whitepaper: "Demand Generation ✅ (prefer other campaigns over this one)" / "X is your variable to A/B test headlines/questions/pains."
- Market Research and Whitepaper: "If you have capital to do so, it is helpful to entice with some type of reward. (Ex. $5 starbucks giftcard)"

**Framework: 7 campaign types (fit: demand gen / demand capture).**
1. Case Study (✅ / ✅ depends on the case study) 2. Risk Reversal (✅ / ✅ if the market is shopping but skeptical) 3. Lead Magnet (✅ / ⚠️ only if the free thing is exactly what they already try to buy) 4. Direct Offer (✅ / ✅ when pain is obvious and outcome simple) 5. Loom Video (✅ / ✅ if tied to something real) 6. Market Research (✅ / ❌ most of the time) 7. Education-Based / Whitepaper (✅ prefer others / ✅).

**Examples (verbatim; Dulcy's own unless noted).**
- Case Study (Dulcy): "we talked about how we built an AI omnichannel outreach system and generated $180,000 in revenue for a client, and offered to send the strategy over what it looked like, and the email copy we used to get the result." (Dulcy claim; client not named in this doc; verify which client and get approval before reuse.)
- Lead Magnet (Dulcy): "For Dulcy, we offered 1,000 free leads as a front-end offer. If someone needs more leads, they most likely need lead generation, simple." Other: "Voice AI: offering a free AI employee for a specific use case" / "Amazon brands: optimize one product for free"
- Direct Offer: “I noticed you were on page two for X products on Amazon, we could get you to #1 in three days.” / “I noticed your company is being outranked by Competitor 1 and Competitor 2 for key terms like X Y Z, would it be useful if I shared how to increase inbound calls from Google?” / “Have you considered using greeting cards with a QR code video attached to win more listings…?”
- Loom: “I bought a jacket from your store… noticed you haven’t tapped into Amazon… I made a short video explaining how you could increase sales by 20% in 2 months, can I send it?” / “I noticed your add-to-cart button isn’t working on mobile, I shot a quick loom showing how to fix it in 5 minutes, can I send it?” / “I made a loom walking through how we target your audience + the positioning + the same email approach we used to generate $180,000, can I send it?”
- Market Research: “Let me preface by saying I’m not trying to sell you anything, can I get your feedback as someone we would normally sell to?” / “What would it take for you to consider an alternative?” / “We’re conducting market research… found competitor insights… happy to share findings.”
- Whitepaper: “We’re writing a whitepaper on X… we’ve interviewed Company, Company, Company… would you be open to a quick conversation?” + "Offer early access to the report, add a link to their site, keep it anonymous if they prefer."

## D25. Highest Converting Follow Up Sequence (Agency Ignition)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1mqgcjKKPf6i8DkqmyIVfhsItbvsbL9msgX1_IW1ZQ6c/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-12-21
- **Author:** Alex (Agency Ignition; "Over the last 4 years I’ve consumed an unimaginable amount of cold email content").

**Summary.** Alex's rules for no-reply follow-up steps, plus 10 follow-up templates with "why I built it / why it worked" notes and an easy-CTA rotation list. Several templates are in N1; the rules, annotations and templates 9-10 are new.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "Step 1 should always be your hardest-hitting email (best hook + most compelling value)."
- "Most sequences should be 2–3 steps."
- "4 steps is rare and usually only makes sense if your TAM is small (think under ~10,000 prospects) and you need to squeeze every reply out of the list."
- "I usually space sends 1–2 days apart."
- "The biggest mistake people make is the lazy follow up: “just bumping this up.” It’s weak, it looks thirsty, and it adds no value."
- "Your follow-ups should do three things: 1. restate the core idea (without copy/pasting the same email) 2. make the “yes” easier each step 3. *Add more context through social proof"
- "Also: test thread reply vs non-thread reply. I’m 50/50 on it, depending on niche + offer."
- "Step 2 should feel like: “same idea, quicker, easier yes.” You can: add context / offer the lead magnet again / offer a Loom instead of a call / give 2–3 specific ideas. Keep CTA’s short and easy: “Can I send more info?”"
- "Step 3 is where you go low friction. Three approaches that work a lot: “Are you the right person?” / “Should this go to you or {COLLEAGUE}?” / Reiterating the offer again"
- "I’ve had plenty of campaigns where Step 2 and Step 3 generated the majority of positive replies."
- "Just don’t do the cringey “last email” thing."
- "The core rule: each step gets easier to say yes to, and adds something new."

**Templates with Alex's annotations (verbatim; good).**
1. Ideas for Employee 1 or 2 (script = N1). Built: "“You or X?” is a built-in pattern interrupt and lowers resistance." / "The 3 ideas prove you didn’t mass blast them (even if they’re templated)." / "The CTA is a simple evaluation question, not “book a call.”" Worked: "People respond faster to pick-from-a-menu questions than open-ended pitches." / "Even a “not us, talk to X” reply is a win, it routes you correctly."
2. We just helped CASE STUDY video explanation (script = N1). Built: "One line of proof, one line CTA. Nothing extra." / "Video CTA is easier than “book time.”" Worked: "The prospect doesn’t have to commit to a meeting to see if it’s real."
3. Leaving Company (trigger-based) (script = N1). Built: "Uses a real-world trigger (job change) without sounding like LinkedIn creepy." / "Frames the problem as something they don’t want to deal with anymore." Worked: "Timing + relevance + low-friction CTA = replies."
4. Wasn’t sure if I should send to you or colleague (problem facing?) (script = N1). Built: "“You or colleague” reduces the pressure of being sold." / "Problem-first framing forces them to self-identify." Worked: "Easy “yes/no” reply, and it qualifies the lead instantly."
5. Wasn’t sure ... (case study) (script = N1). Built: "Same routing mechanic, but now it’s proof-driven instead of problem-driven." Worked: "You’re not asking them to believe you, you’re asking where to send details."
6. Meme follow up (pattern interrupt) - "very rare" (script = N1). Built: "It’s a hard pattern interrupt." / "It feels human (when your ICP matches it)." Worked: "People reply to novelty, especially when the meme nails the pain."
7. Added context (handles objections before they reply) (script = N1). Built: "Removes the “sounds nice but how?” objection." / "Keeps it skimmable (bullets win)." Worked: "It makes the offer feel more “real” without writing an essay."
8. As I mentioned, results reminder (script = N1). Built: "It’s slightly confrontational (in a good way)." / "Forces them to either ignore again or answer the obvious." Worked: "Prospects will often reply just to correct you or clarify, and that’s still a reply."
9. Lead magnet follow up (NOT a thirsty bump) — NEW:
> Hi {FIRST_NAME}, I wanted to see if you had looked at the lead magnet I mentioned. In 15 minutes, we can have the data prepped, show you exactly how we do it, and walk you through the same email flow that helped one of our clients generate $180K in new business.
> Mind if I send over some more info?
Built: "It’s a follow up that adds value instead of “did you see this?”" / "It restates the payoff fast, then asks permission." Worked: "The lead magnet becomes the reason for the follow up, not your need for a reply."
10. “I’ll do the work first” follow up (1,000 verified prospects + video) — NEW, Dulcy's own:
> {Hey|Hi} {FIRST_NAME},
> I’ve been helping B2B Clutch video production agencies build predictable pipelines using an AI-driven omnichannel outreach system.
> To see if we’d actually be useful, I can put together 1,000 verified prospects for {COMPANY} and share a short video showing exactly how we’d approach your offer and niche.
> Would you find that useful?
Built: "It flips the dynamic: you do work first, then ask for permission." / "CTA is a micro-yes." Worked: "Nobody wants a call with a stranger. People will say yes to “send it over” if it feels useful."

Easy CTA rotation (Step 2 -> Step 3), verbatim: Open to hearing more? / Want to see how it works? / Is this a priority right now? / Should I send it here? / Are you the right person for this? / Want me to pass over a quick example? / World’s worst idea to discuss further?

**Conflict to flag.** D25 says space steps 1–2 days apart; D7 (TWM audit) says "4 days a bit long for a sequence break"; N3 (Closers.io) waits 4 then 3 days; D5 (MJJ Instantly) uses 3-7 days. Alex's own rule (D25) = 1–2 days, 2–3 steps.


## D26. Understanding Market Sophistication (Agency Ignition)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1G8CifrOHiwXU8qCOE3Dt287FTLHgKaXI1R4mdn52jhg/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-12-21
- **Author:** Alex (Agency Ignition).

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "Before you touch copy, subject lines, or even your lead list, you need to understand one thing: Your market has already been trained."
- "Cold email can amplify demand. It can’t create demand for something no one cares about."
- PMF signals: "Demand exists: They already spend money trying to solve the problem. / It’s painful: The problem costs them time, money, or growth. / They stick around: When people buy, they don’t immediately vanish." "No PMF? You’ll get “sounds cool” responses but no real conversions."
- Message-market fit: "You can say the value in one line / Your wording matches how they talk / It’s instantly clear who it’s for and what it does"
- "If you can’t explain it simply, your prospects won’t “lean in.” They’ll click off."
- Market sophistication = "“How many times has your prospect heard something like this before?”"
- "If you talk to a Level 4 market like it’s Level 1, you sound naive. If you talk to a Level 1 market like it’s Level 4, you sound confusing and try-hard."

**Framework: 4 sophistication levels (verbatim).**
- Level 1 New Market: "Your job: introduce the concept / Focus: simple education + why it matters"
- Level 2 Aware Market: "Your job: explain your angle / Focus: “why this approach works better”"
- Level 3 Competitive Market: "Your job: prove you’re not the same / Focus: process + proof + what you do differently"
- Level 4 Saturated Market: "Your job: pattern interrupt + credibility / Focus: specific mechanism, constraints (“who it’s for / not for”), and real receipts"

**Example (verbatim; good; illustrative numbers, not proof):** “We help B2B agencies add 10–20 qualified calls a month using cold email + follow-up systems.”

## D27. Using Real Client Wins to Build Instant Trust ("Build Instant Trust with Real Client Wins", Agency Ignition)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1kRiL7MmbsaYW9iFcHD8fssBB4wfjFwXl3kIgF5Bdcd4/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-12-20
- **Author:** Alex (Agency Ignition). Written for VSL scripts but the proof rules apply to email.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "nothing builds trust faster than showing that you’ve already done exactly what they want… for someone just like them."
- "Listening = Copywriting ... Pay attention to your sales calls. Every objection, every concern, every frustration, that’s your goldmine."
- "When you solve one client’s specific issue and another prospect brings up that same pain point? That’s not a coincidence. That’s your script writing itself."
- "Numbers Beat Adjectives ... Specifics = trust."

**Examples (verbatim; illustrative real-estate example, not a Dulcy client).**
- Pain quotes: “The leads ghost after one text.” / “I’m paying $40 for buyers who don’t qualify.” / “I call them and they say, ‘Who are you again?’”
- GOOD line: “If you’re spending thousands a month on Zillow leads who won’t even pick up the phone or have already signed with an agent then we should talk. Last month, we helped a broker in Miami replace 90% of his lead spend with booked appointments from pre-screened buyers.”
- BAD: “We help realtors get more quality leads.” GOOD: “We helped 7 agents close 13 homes last quarter, all from cold traffic turned into live buyer calls.”

## D28. Last Minute Check List (Agency Ignition)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1gBzPqZOW4l0c8_iHOGza205pqZHVdZuHstXqnu-2KwU/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-12-21
- **Author:** Alex (Agency Ignition).

**Summary.** Pre-launch checklist: lead list, script, copy quality, spintax, spam filter, A/B rules, campaign setup, omni-channel readiness, variable preview, gut check.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "This is the final “don’t be an idiot” check before you hit launch."
- Script Check: "I understand what type of campaign this is. / I understand the goal (reply? booking? permission-based CTA? lead magnet acceptance?). / I understand the expected results (what “good” looks like for this campaign type)."
- Copy Quality: "Short, to the point. / Easy to say yes (low resistance CTA). / Sounds like something I’d say in real life. / Ask yourself: “Would I respond to this?” / Send the email to myself and read it at first glance like I’m busy (because prospects are)."
- Spintax: "Confirm spintax is in place where it matters (subject line, key lines, signature if needed). Make sure it still reads normal in every variation."
- Spam: "Run the script through spam filters. Remove/replace obvious triggers and weird phrases. Keep it as close to clean as possible (1–2 flags can be fine)."
- A/B: "Only test ONE variable per 1,000+ leads."
- Setup: "Subject line is engaging (and doesn’t scream “marketing email”). / Nothing is misspelled. / No missing assets that you need (links, lead magnet, doc, Loom, whatever you referenced)."
- Preview: "Before launch, I manually preview 10–15 random leads and check: Variables populate correctly (FIRST_NAME, COMPANY, etc.) / Spintax doesn’t create weird sentences / Nothing looks broken, awkward, or “obviously automated”. If anything looks off here, it will look off at scale."
- "Get colleague feedback on the script (fresh eyes catch dumb mistakes). If it feels even slightly confusing, tighten it."


## D29. What Cold Email Actually Is (and Why It Still Works) (Agency Ignition)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/19H647J-sLScmYyR_8ZhhrJf1IuZ8RR_oOvojhtmWhzo/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-12-21
- **Author:** Alex (Agency Ignition).

**Summary.** Intro module: definition, cold vs warm, why cold email works, who it fits, tool costs, misconceptions, the 3 pillars. Copy-relevant parts only below.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "Cold email is simple: Emailing people that don’t know you to tell them about your thing."
- "Warm leads = email list / Cold leads = lead list. If you treat cold leads like warm leads, you’re going to burn domains and get cooked on deliverability."
- Pipeline math: "If you can book a meeting for every 1,000 emails sent, you need 4 calls to close a deal, and you want to close 5 new clients a month. You just have to send 20,000 emails."
- "Cold email is best for B2B ... not ideal for: low-ticket products / most B2C"
- "on average cold email doesn’t work for 6 out of 10 people… because they fail to understand one of these points" — Pillar 1 Infrastructure, Pillar 2 List Building, Pillar 3 Offer & Copy ("does your offer solve a real problem? / does your offer have value in the market? / Is your strategy unique/interesting? / can you get positive replies? / does it turn into booked meetings of good quality?")
- "If your offer is weak, no subject line on earth will save you."
- "it’s legal when done correctly (CAN-SPAM)."

## D30. Defining Your Cold Outreach Offer (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1KSfakspJ9iofTi5V8WQ3CsUayhJ91-5_m2y3NhwxhDM/edit
- **Last modified:** 2026-01-08
- **Author / owner:** scale@dulcy.io (Alex; has a "Dulcy-specific outbound offer ideas" part). Offer worksheet (Forge work now in Scribe). Hormozi-style (starving crowd, value equation, problem -> solution stack, trim and stack).

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "You’re not “building an agency service.” You’re building an offer people feel dumb saying no to."
- "Stop selling “cold email.” Start selling a clear outcome for a specific person with a low-risk path to get it."
- "If you don’t have a clear offer, your outbound becomes “random outreach.”"
- Starving crowd: "Pain (active problem they admit) / Money (they can actually pay) / Reachability (you can list-build + contact them) / Growth (the category isn’t dying)"
- Niche statement template: "“I help [specific ICP] who struggle with [specific bottleneck] to achieve [specific measurable outcome] using [unique mechanism].”"
- Value equation: "Dream outcome is bigger / Certainty is higher / Time-to-results is shorter / Effort required is lower"
- "Most outbound offers sound like: “We do cold email” / “We send X emails/day” / “We book meetings” That’s commoditized. Your job is to package it like a system with a unique mechanism."
- "People don’t fear paying. They fear paying and getting nothing."
- "You don’t need a wild guarantee. You need a believable one." Examples: "“If we don’t launch by X date, you don’t pay until we do.”" / "“If we don’t generate X qualified conversations by X day, we work free until we hit it.”" / "“If deliverability is below X benchmark, we rebuild infra at no cost.”"
- Next-step CTA template: "If you want, I’ll look at your current outbound and tell you exactly what I’d fix first."
- Launch checklist: "Offer is one sentence clear / ICP is specific / Proof exists (even small proof) / Guarantee is realistic / Boundaries are written / Your CTA is one step / Your onboarding is documented"

**Dulcy-specific offer angles (verbatim).** "Angle 1: “Pipeline in 30 days” - Fast win focus - Strong for founders who are impatient (most are)" / "Angle 2: “Done-with-you system build” - You build the machine + teach them what matters - Strong for operators who want control" / "Angle 3: “Infra + deliverability safety” - Strong for people who already got burned - Lead with prevention + stability" / "Angle 4: “Reply quality > volume” - Strong pattern interrupt - “Less spam, more conversations”"

## D31. Writing Copy That Gets the Right People to Book (Agency Ignition)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1-fXwy-_lopBe5TRZZdZm66loCT6LeNlVVl_0U53K_5M/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-12-20
- **Author:** Alex (Agency Ignition). Landing-page copy lesson (the text returned is cut off at the end, "Like a restaur"). Included for its language rules, which apply to email.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "They sound like the prospects thinking. Basically as the prospect reads they have to imagine it's them speaking to them."
- "It comes from understanding your ICP deeper than anyone else."
- "Use the words your ICP actually uses: patients, leads, bookings, consults, pipeline, appointments, clinicians, whatever’s accurate."
- "Great copy is about resonance, not poetry." / "they aren’t trying to sound smart, they’re trying to sound familiar."
- "Remember people will choose a heart surgeon over a general surgeon for their problem."
- BAD generic lines: “We help you grow your business” / “Get more traffic and sales” / “Scale your company with digital marketing”
- GOOD example (third-party NKP Medical headline): “Attract More Patients and Grow Your Med Spa with Proven Marketing Strategies”

## D33. THE COPYWRITING PLAYBOOK — "The Cold Email Copywriting Playbook for 2025 [INTERNAL USE]" (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1bwyDjnlwhOyVqUys2jnNn71RyKdsy85fyL5jKNU3u-w/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-03-17
- **Author:** Alexander Woung, Dulcy (signed letter at the top). Owner scale@dulcy.io.

**Summary.** Alex's original 2025 internal script collection (the seed of N1). Same five guidelines and mostly the same templates as N1. New items: the opening letter, a restaurant subject-line + P.S. pairing, and a filled-in example of "Need more ICP leads?".

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- Letter: "Over the past couple of years running my agency, I’ve seen a ton of different cold email scripts and angles. Some crushed it, getting meetings left and right. Others? Not so much. This playbook is a collection of every script that works when used properly. No fluff, just the stuff that works."
- Guidelines: "No fluff / Easy to say yes to / Offer so good they feel stupid to say no / Low CTA friction / Less than 50 words"

**New examples (verbatim; good).**
> SUBJECT: {{local restaurant)}'s cocktails - yes or no?
> {FIRST_NAME} - could {COMPANY} use more {ICP} leads each month?
> Only asking because {Distinct value prop with social proof}.
> Worth a chat?
> PS - I’m sure you get a million cold emails/day, so I thought the subject line might stand out to break the ice!

Filled example (illustrative; "123 marketing" is a placeholder, not a named client):
> Nick - could ABC Marketing use more health tech start up leads each month?
> Only asking because we helped 123 marketing generate 14 last month on a pay-per-lead basis.

Subject Lines/PS Lines: "- {{famous local menu item 1}} or {{famous local menu item 21}" / "- {{local restaurant)}'s cocktails - yes or no?"

Variant of "Leaving Company Stoked" with a P.S. opt-out (verbatim):
> Hey {FIRSTNAME} - I’d have to imagine you didn’t leave {past company} stoked to worry about {problem}.
> {Messaging to be tested}
> No need to hop on a call, could I send a video of how it works?
> Signature
> P.S. Or feel free to tell me to pound sand…

The doc ends with empty headings "MARKETING AGENCIES / SCIRPT ! / SCRIPT 2 / SAAS" (unfinished).

## D35. How To Create Campaign (AI) — "Cold Email Copywriting SECRETS" (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1kqOnixN66sduun4d5yC1H3EagcGKhU0_CArJek5X9JA/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-09-10
- **Author / owner:** scale@dulcy.io (Alex; early version of D21 "3 Simple Rules").

**Summary.** "The Triple Tap": three actions a lead must take (open, read, act). Earlier numbers than D21: 6 sentences max and 6th-grade reading level (D21, later: 4 sentences / 50-75 words / 4th grade; N2: below 3rd grade).

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- "This is NOT normal copywriting / Using the wrong word can land you in spam / Your goal is NOT to sell / Your goal is to identify if your lead has a problem you can solve / Remember, they don’t know you, trust you, and are NOT looking for your solution. / GOAL: Make it sound like an email to a friend"
- "The Triple Tap: 3 separate actions your lead must take to give you a positive reply ... First: They must open the email. This is done in the preview text. Second: They must read the email. You have moments to catch their attention and build trust. Third: They must take action. This is your CTA."
- "Preview Text= the subject line and the 1st line of the email / Determines the open rate (Use reply rate as a proxy)! / Should be related to the email content, but NEVER signal selling / Goal is for the email to sound like it could be from a client or vendor / Questions get opens (we can't help it)"
- "Get them to reply with one word! (no links) / Don’t make them think!"
- DOs: "Keep it short (6 sentences max) / 6th grade reading level / Tone should match the profile of your ICP (Cosmetics owner vs dealership owner) / Low-resistance CTA / Establish Credibility (case studies, media, testimonials) / Be SPECIFIC about the problem and solution"
- DONTs: "NEVER overpromise / NEVER be vague about what you do / NEVER confuse the lead / NEVER bait and switch (Use baiting wisely) / NEVER use promotional or salesy language"

**Examples (verbatim; good).**
- Subject: Sending cold emails? / "Not sure if you are sending these or not [name]. Mind checking?"
- Subject: Is this you on google? / "Just came across this on google, can you confirm if this is you or not?"
- Body: "I’m actually running the outbound marketing for [well known competitor]. After seeing their (pretty crazy) success I was hoping I could do the same for you. Here’s an article that USA Today wrote about my methods. If you already have more qualified calls than you can handle, then ignore me. But if you’re interested in building an outbound machine, I’d love to show you the strategy."
- CTA: "Would it be cool if I sent over the strategy?"

Note: D7 later says "“I’d love to” ... prospect get the “ick” from it". The body example above uses "I’d love to show you"; prefer the D7 rule.

## D37. Cold Email Examples.docx (Closers.io)

- **Link:** https://drive.google.com/file/d/12194lRqNt0UJ8uYztU08Lc9vwlMewzeJ/view (PDF twin 1HFItkZS7IjQFcm9T5H3UABmUnS2HyEbT)
- **Last modified:** 2025-12-13 (upload date; content ~2022)
- **Author:** Closers.io / STA (senders "Mitchell", "Jared").

**Summary.** Five versions of STA's appointment-setter cold email: personalized first line + "do you have a sales team" question + case study + CTA (reply "1", reply with cell #, or watch VSL) + long track-record P.S.

**Label.** Third-party; old-school (long, big-name-drop P.S., link CTAs). Against current Dulcy rules: links in first touch, >75 words. Useful pattern: question + "The reason I ask is..." + case study + one-word reply CTA.

**Examples (verbatim).**

Example 1. Subject: feedback about your sales team
> David - saw what you’re doing with your creative agency on facebook the other day and wanted to reach out personally. Do you currently have a sales team that handles your enrollment?
> The reason I ask is that we were able to go from 250k/m --> 1.9M/m in 7 months using 100% commission-based appointment setters. We were also able to replicate those results for the Beauty Boss (30k/m --> 450k/m in 60 days). I’ll post the rest of our track record in the PS below.
> If this interests you… reply “1” to this email and I’ll send you a 3-minute video that will detail everything.
> Thanks
> Mitchell
> PS: Here’s a small fraction of clients we’ve worked with: - Tony Robbins - Frank Kern - Dean Graziosi - Agora Financial - Jay Abraham - Rich Schefren - Pete Vargas - Todd Brown - Jeff Lerner - Tanner Chidester - Client & Community - Aleric Heck - Joel Kaplan - Astroflipping & Sub2 - Self Publishing School ...among 450+ others. Reply and let me know if you want the video from me detailing how this works.

Example 2
> Hey {{first:there}}, I was checking out your {{platform:Facebook page}} and heard about what you are doing with {{company:your business}} and I was wondering if you currently have a sales team that handles your client enrollment?
> The reason I ask is that we were able to go from 250k/m --> 1.9M/m in 7 months using a VSL and commission-based appointment setters. We were also able to replicate those results for the Beauty Boss (30k/m --> 450k/m in 60 days). I’ll post the rest of the track record below in the PS.
> Is this top of mind for you right now? If so shoot me a reply with your cell # and I can give you further details.
> - Mitchell
> PS: [same track-record list as Example 1, without "...among 450+ others"]

Example 3 (same body as Example 2, VSL CTA)
> Is this top of mind for you right now? If so, here's a quick video on how the offer works. [link]
> Check it out then schedule a demo using the link below the video and I can break down how this would work for your business specifically.
> - Mitchell
> PS: [track-record list]

Example 4 (first line as a variable with fallback)
> {{firstline:Hey - I was checking out your Facebook and noticed the work you do in the space and wanted to reach out personally and ask if you currently have a sales team that handles your client enrollment?}}
> [same case-study paragraph, VSL CTA, signed "- Jared", track-record P.S.]

Example 5
> {{firstline:Hey - I was checking out your Facebook and noticed the work you do in the space and wanted to reach out personally and ask if you are potentially looking for any appointment setters to help you generate more sales calls?}}
> [same case-study paragraph, VSL CTA, signed "- Jared", track-record P.S.]

Pattern note: variables with fallback text, e.g. `{{first:there}}`, `{{platform:Facebook page}}`, `{{company:your business}}`, `{{firstline:...}}`.
