## D39. Testing Guidelines.docx and KPIs For Email.docx (Closers.io)

- **Links:** Testing https://drive.google.com/file/d/11tgD1vTYrRf-lKZYaRnR4WNdSug2uucs/view (PDF 1YWic_CejWgs2Dd8aQ8abeE-FkpL1sLfA); KPIs https://drive.google.com/file/d/1vAxsSJ9GivYosvvNRvVfzA2wF6mDXdpE/view (PDF 1aXtf1xJ7ogsMcCposb_juj1pC9FrCDZU). Modified 2025-12-13. Author Closers.io.

**Testing rules (verbatim).** "100 unique sends PER test / You can start with two test… once each hits 100, then make a call / Keep in mind, if your opens are abysmal and your 30-40 emails in you may wanna take an extra look ... in terms of response definitely wait until 100. / Test one variable at a time! / First lines / Question ending the first line / Case Study / CTA / Track records / Subject line / Biggest levers… / Quality of first line / The initial question / The quality / relevancy of the case studies / The CTA"

Conflict: Closers.io tests at 100 sends; Alex's rules test one variable per 1,000+ leads (D28) or 2k+ (N13); Isaiah 250 leads per variant (N12). Use Alex's.

**KPIs (verbatim; Closers.io's own 2022 numbers, not Dulcy benchmarks).** "Open Rates (Total Opens / Total Send): 40% (both unique and total) / Open Rates (Campaign Level) 70% / Click Thru Rate (Clicks / Opens) 9% / Reply rate (Replies / Unique Opens) 23.98% / Set rate (sets / unique sends) 2.27% (5.58% per open) / Closing rate (closes / total sets) 29.7%"

## D40. 1.3 - Creating Your Copy Platform.docx (Closers.io "Copy Platform - Advanced")

- **Link:** https://drive.google.com/file/d/1i4WC8jfgXUpbKmuHVgV5hH2KObk567Z9/view (PDF 1bt_XjZZAPAuKLUYGKSNdWCaTPWwvQazH)
- **Last modified:** 2025-12-12
- **Author:** Closers.io (Cole Gordon's team; examples for their STA and RCA offers). Third-party direct-response framework. Read in full.

**Summary.** A copy-platform worksheet that sits upstream of any email: headlines by awareness (Schwartz), appeal, hook, promise, wants/needs/features/benefits/costs, USP, "Big Four" (New/Only/Big/Fast/Easy/Safe/Predictable/Simple), "Why Cubed", Blair Warren elements, claims and proof, bullseye clients, ideal self, what stops them, assumptions, insight, desire, new opportunity, mechanism, marketing thesis, belief ladder, reasons why, DIY/competitor options, what they won't give up, common enemy, jealousy, validation, objections (internal/external/vehicle), "what can't you fix that you can celebrate", 3 problems, symptomatic pain list, visions list, reading list.

**Rules / frameworks (exact quotes).**
- Schwartz: "if your prospect is aware of your product and realizes it can satisfy his desire, your headline starts with your product. If he is not aware of your product, but only the desire itself, your headline starts with the desire. If he is not yet aware of what he really seeks, but is concerned only with the general problem, your headline starts with that problem and crystalizes it into a specific need - Eugene Schwartz"
- Headline components: "Ultra-Specific / Unique / Useful / Urgent / Simplicity / Trendy / Does the headline seed a story? / Spark curiosity? / Drill home a primary benefit? / Feel like breaking news? / Imply ease or speed?"
- Appeal: "Decide Your Appeal – “the reason you give the reader for buying” ... Sex appeal ... Greed ... Fear ... Duty / honor / professionalism ... Appeals are conveyed through emotions – your goal is to make the prospect FEEL something."
- Hook: "the element of your marketing message that peaks your prospects attention & gives them a reason to continue consuming your message!" ... "Remember: You know what NOT to say based on your competitors, and what TO say based on your marketplace sophistication." Construction: "Call to audience: Did you know / If you / Are you / For __ / Specificity of issue / circumstance / situation ... / Revelation of new idea / concept / vehicle". Example: "If you (or your sales reps) are letting costly deals fall through the cracks... there’s a new way of selling that allows you to systematically remove your prospects objections before the close so that you’re no longer leaving easy money on the table."
- Promise: "NOT the big benefit of your PRODUCT! The big benefit of them CONSUMING YOUR MESSAGE"
- Features -> benefit -> benefit of benefit -> cost -> cost of cost (worked example with "irresistible and economically scalable offer").
- USP: "Three aspects of the USP: Uniqueness (must SEEM or BE unique) / Usefulness ... / Conceptual simplicity ... / Bonus - Trending." Examples: “This print-ai tracking script reduces your ad costs by 52% or your money back” / “We’ll train you in sales and work with you until you get your first sale - guaranteed”
- "Why Cubed ... 1. Why should I listen to you? 2. Create your epiphany story... 3. What’s in it for me? 4. Why now?"
- Blair Warren: "People will do anything for those who encourage their dreams, justify their failures, allay their fears, confirm their suspicions, and help them throw rocks at their enemies"
- Proof: "Case studies. Better if it’s someone LIKE THEM / More senses, better the proof (direct in-person referral > documentary > video > written)" ... "Specificity: The more specific the claim, the more believable. We made 1000s vs we made $1347"
- Marketing thesis template: "If I can make people believe that (NEW OPPORTUNITY / METHOD / CATEGORY) is the key to (DESIRE) and they are only obtainable through (MECHANISM) then all other objections and concerns become irrelevant and they buy." "In most cases, your MARKETING (think the VSL) makes sale #1 and your sales call makes sale #2."
- Objections: "Internal ... External ... Vehicle ... Why not from YOU"
- Symptomatic pain list: "write out hooks based on what your prospects are experiencing. For ease… I like to write them out in the form of a question." Examples (STA): "Sales rep miss a sale now you would be closed? / No more white space on your calendar? / Did you fire another sales rep? / Sales team fall into another slump? / Feel like you're stuck in strategy session penitentiary? / Can’t seem to find good sales reps?"
- Reading list: Expert Secrets (Brunson); 13 word sales letter (Albuquerque); Great Leads, Ready Fire Aim (Masterson); E5 masterclass (Todd Brown); Take my money (Milligan); Social Media Warfare (Mitch Miller); One sentence persuasion (Blair Warren); Pitch Anything & Flip The Script (Klaff); How to write a good advertisement (Schwab); Breakthrough Advertising (Schwartz).

Use for Scribe: the "symptomatic pain list as questions" and the marketing-thesis template are directly usable to write poke-the-bear first lines; matches D23/D13.

- ">ESP MATCHING / >1 WORD UNSUB / >Spam words (dont use company name or exact name" — sender-name variants listed: "AW / A Woung / W. / Alec Woung / Alex Wong / Dulcy Media / Dulcy / Dulcy Lead Gen / Dulcy B2B / Dulcy LG"
- ">Dont let them know its a cold email until they open oit (stop bs subject line and first sentence"
- "Preview text determines open rate / Include a pattern interrupt / Say something that triggers curiosity / Start with something different / Include a question (ai prompt of name competitor?)"
- "Lead magnet: They should be good enough that others would pay for them"
- Draft Email 1A (verbatim, Dulcy, good): "Would it be okay if I created you a list of 3k leads for nothing."
- Recipient provider mix in a list (Alex's data point): "17226 google 57.61% / 8994 outlook 30.08% / 3679 other 12.30%".

## D48. Email Content.docx (two identical copies) and Email content for Alex.docx (Word files)

- **Links:** https://drive.google.com/file/d/1-6dN1M1nStVdGwTJNFOxzQj_yT7eP9X2/view and copy 1kjEAhVbfwq2l8DHfwST581YJp8O8pr1i (2025-04-14); subset "Email content for Alex.docx" 1lm6OhVdeBRfbSKjzWTGnoBRVDqU7RSIR (2025-04-09, first 5 emails only). Owner scale@dulcy.io. Author UNKNOWN (written for a mortgage broker referred to as "Mitch"; Australian market: "Aussies", "First Home Guarantee").
- **Type:** nurture / list emails (case-study stories and market-trend updates by segment: first-home buyers, upgraders, investors, self-employed, commercial). **Not cold outbound.** Past client, not one of the current six. CLIENT-SPECIFIC; never reuse its stories or stats.
- **Structure (pattern worth keeping).** Title "How We Helped [Name] [Outcome]" -> "Meet [Name] — [situation]" -> "That’s where we stepped in." -> "we helped by:" 3 bullets -> "Now [Name] ..." result -> quote -> "If you're [in a similar position], ..." soft close. Alternating "Market Trends" emails with 3 starred stats.
- **One example (verbatim, label: client nurture copy, good structure):**
> Hi There,
> Meet Daniel — a savvy investor who already owned two properties but was struggling to get finance approved for his next move. He was hitting borrowing limits and banks were rejecting his applications despite strong equity and rental income.
> That’s where we stepped in.
> By restructuring his portfolio and presenting a stronger lending case, we helped by:
> • Consolidating debt to improve serviceability
> • Refinancing one property to access equity
> • Securing pre-approval with a lender who supports portfolio growth
> Now Daniel has added a third property to his portfolio in a high-growth area — and has a clear plan for property number four.
> “I knew I had the equity, I just needed someone who knew how to unlock it. Mitch and the team understood my goals and helped me grow without hitting a wall.”
> Daniel
> If you're ready to expand your investment portfolio but feel stuck, we can help you take the next step.
- The other 14 emails follow the same two patterns (titles: How Alan and Shannon Bought Their First Home with Only a $13,000 Deposit; First Home Buyer Market Trends You Should Know; How We Helped Bianca Buy Her first Home; How We Helped a Family Upgrade Without Selling First; Property Market Update: What’s Changing for Homeowners; How We Helped Josh & Emma Upgrade Without Hassle; How We Helped an Investor Add $500K in Portfolio Value; Investment Property Trends: What’s Coming Next; How a Business Owner Got a $1.5M Loan with No Tax Returns; What’s Changing for Self-Employed Borrowers?; How We Helped Laura Get Approved as a Business Owner; How We Helped Sam Secure a 30 year $3M Commercial Loan; Business Lending Trends: What You Need to Know; How We Helped James Secure a $3M Business Loan). Not copied in full because they are warm nurture copy for a past non-current client. Note a copy error in the source: email 4's quote is signed "Alan and Shannon" instead of Lucy and Brett.


## D49. Campaign Copy to Review — Aline / Reach Digital, Aug 10, 2023 (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1zNvhdgZsY2qrzAyO2Dw1FbM9Gnt7Wq70fuQBTFCjGAU/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-07-25 (content dated Aug 10, 2023)
- **Author / owner:** jdg@getdealflow.ca (Deal Flow, a third-party agency; shared with Dulcy). Copy for that agency's 2023 clients Reach Digital (DTC paid-ads growth partner) and Aline (AI contract-management software). Not Dulcy clients now.

**Summary.** Five Smartlead variants for each of two campaigns, with ICP/job titles.

**Label: BAD by Dulcy's rules (useful as anti-examples).** All are 70-120+ words in one block, self-focused ("We on the other hand are actually a strategic growth partner"), stack many claims and brand names, and end with a hard ask ("Got 15 minutes to experience the future?"). Breaks D21 (4 sentences, low-friction CTA), D23 ("Talking about yourself too much", "Do you have 30 minutes" type CTAs), D7 ("We specialize in..." commodity lines). Subject lines "{{firstName}}, thoughts?", "Quick question", "{{first name}}?" and blank subject are in line with Dulcy's internal-email rule.

## D50. ShopInnovator_ColdEmails.docx — "Shop Innovator: 10 Cold Email Examples" (Word file)

- **Link:** https://drive.google.com/file/d/1pqFHze_ioGVhC7Hz143uEblum2SCwfgi/view
- **Last modified:** 2026-03-02
- **Author / owner:** scale@dulcy.io (Dulcy-drafted for client; sender "Dorit, Shop Innovator"; references "Igor" from the discovery call and "the SOP's 'attach to their bet' principle").
- **Client:** Shop Innovator (past Dulcy client, not one of the six current). CLIENT-SPECIFIC: Fire Pit Outfitter and Planta Greenhouses are Shop Innovator's case studies; never reuse.

**Summary.** Ten first-touch emails for high-ticket Shopify "bricks & clicks" home-and-garden retailers, grouped into four offer types (Bet-on-You / free video audit; Problem Diagnosis; Third-Party Authority; Investment Bridge), each with a "WHY THIS WORKS" note. The best-annotated client copy set found.

**Framework (verbatim legend).** "Bet-on-You Emails 1–2 | Problem Diagnosis Emails 3–5 | Third-Party Authority Emails 6–7 | Investment Bridge Emails 8–10". Investment Bridge = "Attaches the offer to an investment the prospect is already making (paid traffic) — following the SOP's 'attach to their bet' principle exactly." (Matches D7 "Investment Piece" and D16 "Investment (I noticed)".)

**Label.** Good per Dulcy's later rules on relevance, specificity, investment-piece hooks and soft CTAs; most bodies run 90-130 words, longer than the 50-75 target (D21/D7). Email 6 ends with a 15-minute ask (harder than preferred). Status: draft examples (approval UNKNOWN).

**Examples (verbatim, with the source's "WHY THIS WORKS").**

EMAIL 1 · BET-ON-YOU / FREE VIDEO AUDIT. SUBJECT: quick thought on your site, {{FirstName}}
> Hi {{FirstName}},
> Noticed you're running a Shopify store for {{CompanyName}} — looked through it for a few minutes.
> You're clearly investing in building something premium. The product quality comes through. But a few things on the site are working against you when it comes to converting high-ticket buyers — the kind of person dropping $3K–$10K without walking into a showroom first.
> I put together a short video walking through exactly what I spotted and what I'd change. No pitch, no fluff — just specific observations about your store.
> Want me to send it over?
> Dorit / Shop Innovator
WHY: "Opens by showing she already spent time on their store — earns instant credibility. 'Premium product quality comes through' respects what they've built before pointing at a gap. The video audit is the intro offer: tiny commitment, high perceived value. Ends with a one-line yes/no ask."

EMAIL 2 · BET-ON-YOU / FREE VIDEO AUDIT. SUBJECT: {{CompanyName}} — spotted something on your product pages
> Hi {{FirstName}},
> Came across {{CompanyName}} while researching high-ticket home and garden brands on Shopify.
> Your product photos are strong. But your product pages aren't doing the heavy lifting they need to for someone considering a $5,000+ purchase online — there's no real trust architecture: no customer story, no 'why us' layer, no objection handling in the copy.
> That gap is where most of your visitors are leaking out.
> I record short personalized video audits for stores like yours — usually 8–10 minutes walking through exactly what I'd fix and why. Takes zero time on your end to receive it.
> Worth sending over?
> Dorit / Shop Innovator
WHY: "Leads with a specific, observable flaw ('trust architecture') that only someone who understands high-ticket buying behavior would name. Frames the problem as a gap between product quality and site quality — a tension the owner already feels. The ask is framed as zero effort on their part."

EMAIL 3 · PROBLEM DIAGNOSIS. SUBJECT: your Klaviyo probably isn't set up for a $4K buyer
> Hi {{FirstName}},
> Most Shopify email flows are built for impulse buyers — short windows, discount-heavy, quick-close logic.
> That works fine when you're selling $40 candles. It doesn't work when your average order is $4,000+.
> For high-ticket buyers, the consideration window is weeks, sometimes months. If your flows aren't nurturing across that timeline — educating, building trust, handling objections — you're leaving a lot of revenue on the table from people who were actually interested.
> I took a look at {{CompanyName}}'s site. I have a few specific thoughts on where your email setup is likely costing you.
> Happy to share them — no strings attached.
> Dorit / Shop Innovator
WHY: "The subject line does the work — it names a specific mismatch the prospect has probably never framed this way. The body educates first, then creates relevance by referencing their actual store. 'No strings attached' lowers the barrier without sounding like a guarantee."

EMAIL 4 · PROBLEM DIAGNOSIS. SUBJECT: one thing I keep seeing on premium Shopify stores
> Hi {{FirstName}},
> The single most common issue I see with high-ticket Shopify brands: the website looks like a catalog, not a showroom.
> It displays the product. It doesn't sell the experience. And for someone considering a $6,000 purchase without being able to touch or see it in person — that's a meaningful difference.
> {{CompanyName}} has the product quality. I'm not sure the site is communicating that yet.
> I work specifically with bricks-and-clicks retailers in home and garden — brands that sell premium products both in-store and online. This gap is almost universal at your stage, and it's fixable.
> I have a few specific thoughts on your store if you're open to hearing them.
> Dorit / Shop Innovator
WHY: "The 'catalog vs. showroom' framing is language the prospect will immediately recognize from their own gut feeling about their site. It names the problem without being condescending. The ICP specificity ('bricks-and-clicks, home and garden') signals that this isn't a generic pitch — she knows their world."

EMAIL 5 · PROBLEM DIAGNOSIS. SUBJECT: {{FirstName}} — your conversion rate is probably fine. here's the problem.
> Hi {{FirstName}},
> A 0.8–1.2% conversion rate on a high-ticket Shopify store isn't necessarily broken — it's expected.
> But here's what most people miss: for a store selling $3K–$15K products, you don't need to double your traffic. You need to convert the people already arriving. Going from 0.9% to 1.8% is the difference between your current revenue and twice your current revenue — from the same ad spend.
> The bottleneck is almost never traffic. It's the trust gap between landing on the page and deciding to buy.
> I work with premium retailers in exactly your position. I looked at {{CompanyName}} — I think I know where the gap is.
> Would it be useful if I shared what I found?
> Dorit / Shop Innovator
WHY: "Uses a reframe the prospect hasn't heard — makes conversion math feel concrete and achievable. Removes the anxiety of 'we need more traffic' and replaces it with 'we need to fix what's already there.' The 0.9% to 1.8% example mirrors Igor's exact conversation from the discovery call — this is the kind of insight that earns trust."

EMAIL 6 · THIRD-PARTY AUTHORITY. SUBJECT: what Fire Pit Outfitter did differently
> Hi {{FirstName}},
> A client of ours — Fire Pit Outfitter, premium outdoor fire features out of Colorado — came to us with a similar setup to {{CompanyName}}: strong product, decent traffic, weak conversions, and an email list that wasn't doing much.
> Their challenge was a brand experience that didn't match the quality of what they sell. Site looked fine. But it wasn't building the kind of trust that moves a $5K purchase.
> We reworked their homepage structure, rebuilt their Klaviyo flows around a longer buying cycle, and developed messaging that protects their MAP pricing without killing conversion.
> I took a look at {{CompanyName}}. I think there's a similar opportunity here.
> Happy to walk you through what we found — takes 15 minutes.
> Dorit / Shop Innovator
WHY: "Uses a real client case study positioned as 'a brand like yours' rather than 'look what we achieved.' The parallel situation ('similar setup') makes the outcome feel transferable and believable. Specific detail (MAP pricing, Klaviyo, homepage) signals genuine expertise. Low-ask close: 15 minutes."

EMAIL 7 · THIRD-PARTY AUTHORITY. SUBJECT: how Planta Greenhouses fixed their conversion problem
> Hi {{FirstName}},
> Planta Greenhouses sells high-ticket residential greenhouses online — products that take months of consideration before purchase.
> Their traffic was solid. Their email list wasn't converting it. The site showed the product, but it didn't make someone feel confident enough to spend $8,000 without seeing it in person.
> We built them a customer story framework — real buyers, real installs, real outcomes — and rebuilt their email flows to match the actual consideration timeline of their buyers. Revenue from email became meaningful within a quarter.
> I bring this up because {{CompanyName}} has a similar dynamic: premium product, high price point, online buyer who can't walk into your showroom.
> The trust-building approach that worked for Planta is directly transferable.
> Want me to show you how it applies to your store?
> Dorit / Shop Innovator
WHY: "Leads with the client's situation, not the result — makes the story feel like a parallel rather than a brag. 'Customer story framework' is specific enough to be credible. The close explicitly bridges the parallel to the prospect's own situation, making the ask feel logical rather than salesy."

EMAIL 8 · INVESTMENT BRIDGE. SUBJECT: you're already spending on traffic — this is the missing piece
> Hi {{FirstName}},
> If you're running Google or Meta ads for {{CompanyName}}, you're already paying to bring high-intent buyers to your site.
> The math on high-ticket paid traffic is brutal if the site doesn't convert: every click from someone who could have spent $5K–$15K and didn't is an expensive miss.
> For premium Shopify brands, the issue is almost never the ads. It's what happens after the click — the trust signals, the product page structure, the email follow-up sequence for people who don't buy on visit one.
> That's the part most PPC agencies don't touch. And it's where the real leverage is.
> I have a specific read on {{CompanyName}}'s store. Happy to share what I think is worth fixing first.
> Dorit / Shop Innovator
WHY: "Attaches the offer to an investment the prospect is already making (paid traffic) — following the SOP's 'attach to their bet' principle exactly. Positions Shop Innovator as complementary to their current ad agency, not competitive. This directly mirrors how Igor originally engaged with Dorit."

EMAIL 9 · INVESTMENT BRIDGE. SUBJECT: {{FirstName}} — your SEO is doing its job. is the site?
> Hi {{FirstName}},
> Looks like {{CompanyName}} has solid organic visibility — good product category pages, reasonable search presence for your niche.
> That's years of work. The question is whether the site is converting that traffic at the rate it should be for a high-ticket brand.
> For most premium retailers, the SEO does its job. The site doesn't. Someone lands on a product page for a $7,000 item and there's not enough there to make them feel confident — no brand story, no customer validation, no trust layering that matches the price point.
> The result: decent traffic, frustrating conversion rate, and no clear answer on why.
> I looked at {{CompanyName}}'s setup. I think I can tell you exactly where the drop-off is happening.
> Worth a conversation?
> Dorit / Shop Innovator
WHY: "Opens by complimenting the investment they've made in SEO — earns goodwill immediately. Then pivots to the gap without criticizing the SEO work. 'Trust layering' is specific vocabulary that signals expertise. Ends with a diagnostic promise: 'I can tell you exactly where the drop-off is.' That's compelling to someone who's frustrated but doesn't know why."

EMAIL 10 · INVESTMENT BRIDGE. SUBJECT: your Klaviyo list is an asset you're probably underusing
> Hi {{FirstName}},
> Building an email list as a high-ticket retailer takes real time — customers don't give their email lightly when the products they're browsing cost thousands of dollars.
> If {{CompanyName}} has even 2,000–5,000 subscribers, that list is worth significantly more than most Klaviyo setups are extracting from it.
> The issue with high-ticket email: standard flows are built for low-consideration buyers. Welcome series that push too fast, abandoned cart sequences that feel cheap, campaigns that don't account for a 60–90 day buying cycle.
> Most of the email revenue is sitting in the gaps — the people who were interested six months ago and never got nurtured properly.
> I'd love to take a look at your current Klaviyo setup and tell you what I see. No commitment, no pitch — just a read on whether there's something worth fixing.
> Dorit / Shop Innovator
WHY: "Opens by validating the work they've already done to build the list — makes the prospect feel understood, not criticized. The '60–90 day buying cycle' detail signals genuine knowledge of high-ticket eCommerce. 'Revenue sitting in the gaps' creates urgency without fabricating a deadline. Closes with a problem diagnosis offer framed as a pure gift."

## D52. RMG copy 1 (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1fGN1KVOYFxTMjZMOsyBDYVt_Gbccv9JNPuGIS_6hxQ8/edit (2025-11-13, owner scale@dulcy.io). Client/prospect RMG Advertising (see Drive deck "Dulcy AI Sales Machine for RMG Advertising"). Not a current client. CLIENT-SPECIFIC claims (CPA ~85% cut, 0.5–0.8% CTR) must not be reused. Approval UNKNOWN.
- **Label:** decent structure (leak hook, Q4 timing, video micro-CTA); Step 1 has no greeting; claims unverified.

STEP 1
> Targeting leak
> Most planners/lenders overspend on ads because platforms target people who “might be interested,” not people actually showing financial intent.
> RMG fixes that using real-world behaviour data (where people go, meet, shop, inquire), so budgets stop leaking into low-intent audiences.
> With Q4 tightening and acquisition costs rising, even small targeting shifts can create fast, measurable lift.
> Want a 1-min video showing how we cut CPA ~85% for a real estate client?
STEP 2
> RMG’s predictive targeting regularly hits 0.5–0.8% CTR vs the 0.15–0.25% industry norm.
> But the bigger win is attribution; most teams scale Q4 budgets without knowing which audiences actually convert, and that’s where money disappears.
> If you want to see how behaviour-based targeting could impact your Q4/Q1 funnel, I can send the short video.
> It highlights the exact gaps most firms don’t notice. Want it?
STEP 3
> Hey {{first_name}},
> The video breaks down how planners/lenders are tightening attribution, reducing wasted spend, and improving acquisition costs using real-world data.
> As Q4 tightens and Q1 budgets lock in, it’s a sharp 1-min breakdown of the easiest places to recover wasted spend. Worth a glance?


## D53. [Retail Media Group] - BEST REPLIES (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1DrEsI9HXKN3jlqCVb5uWVx4zfYObyTH7UtO36XwntH0/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-10-10
- **Author / owner:** scale@dulcy.io (Dulcy; shared 2025-12-05). Senders in copy: Dean Jager and Sumaan Khan, RMG Advertising.
- **Client:** Retail Media Group / RMG Advertising (past client or pilot; not one of the six current). CLIENT-SPECIFIC (GeoQuest Targeting, Predictive Targeting, Amsterdam 150K-visitors case).

**Why it matters.** This is the only "best performing emails" record found: real variants labelled with their reply counts. **Label: GOOD (best performers by replies, per Dulcy's own tracking).** Common traits: one-sentence permission question offering a specific free asset (breakdown, report, device list, case study, forecast, demo), then one sentence of mechanism + proof, spun greeting and sign-off.

**Examples (verbatim, with the source's performance labels).**

Travel & Tourism (Destination Marketing Organizations)
- "1 Interested / Step 1 / Variant 4". Subject: {Quick question {FIRST_NAME}|Question {FIRST_NAME}}
> Hey (FIRST_NAME),
> Could I send a quick breakdown of the top 3 untapped international markets most likely to travel to (CITY) in the next 6 months?
> Our GeoQuest Targeting data looks at where travelers actually go (airports, hotels, attractions) and shows which audiences are most likely to revisit. It’s helped destinations like Amsterdam redirect over 150K visitors beyond the crowded city center.
- "11 Unique replies / The one that precedes the step one / Variant one"
> (Hey/Hi) {FIRST_NAME}, would it be useful if I sent you a free report showing which international visitors are most likely to return to {CITY} this season, based on real-world travel patterns?
> {Best regards/Kind regards/Warm regards/Cheers/Sincerely/Thank you/Thanks},
> Dean Jager
> RMG Advertising
> p.s. A recent campaign in Amsterdam generated 150K+ visits across 10 attractions by targeting segments like “European City Creatives.”
- "7 UNIQUE REPLIES / STEP 1 / VARIANT 7"
> (Hey/Hi) (FIRST_NAME),
> Would you like me to send you an audience device list of travelers who attended major cultural events near (CITY) last year, ready to be retargeted now?
> Our GeoQuest Targeting data looks at where travelers actually go (airports, hotels, attractions), with 3ft precision, and shows which audiences are most likely to revisit. It’s helped destinations like Amsterdam redirect over 150K visitors beyond the crowded city center.
- "7 UNIQUE REPLIES / STEP 1 / VARIANT 8"
> Could I share a quick example of how we helped a city shift tourist traffic away from overcrowded areas toward sustainable attractions? That might be worth considering for (CITY).
> Our GeoQuest Targeting data looks at where travelers actually go (airports, hotels, attractions) and shows which audiences are most likely to revisit. It’s helped destinations like Amsterdam redirect over 150K visitors beyond the crowded city center.
> (Best regards/Kind regards/Warm regards/Cheers/Sincerely/Thank you/Thanks),
> Dean Jager
> RMG Advertising

Online Gaming & Sports Betting

> We use Predictive Targeting to identify high-intent players before an ad is even served, which helps reduce wasted spend.

> Sumaan Khan
> RMG Advertising

Note: the source mixes spintax syntaxes ("(Hey/Hi)", "{a|b}", "(a|b)"); only `{a|b}` works in the sequencer. A source typo "Hey/Hi)" is kept.

## D54. OutboundContent.com (Google Doc, Nick Abraham / Leadbird)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1N1OW5CPKO1IQu-VhAcu6ZixwlBT0GyyVC8SXBbZss6g/edit
- **Last modified:** 2026-02-12
- **Author / owner:** nick@leadbird.io (Nick Abraham, Leadbird). Shared with Dulcy 2026-03-10. Third-party. 0% overlap with the export.
- **Read:** the whole text (~109k characters; the file is 58MB with images that were not readable). Copy-relevant sections quoted below; the rest is agency operations (client portal, hiring 50+ people, Slack vs Front, managing 1,000+ clients, MasterInbox, Cleverly investment, personal year review): not copy, skipped.

**Copywriting rules (exact quotes, "Managing Deliverability In 2026" §4).**
- "The message itself matters, but not in the way most people think."
- "Spintax Everything: Randomize even small details like signatures to create natural variation."
- "Keep It Simple: No images, PDFs, or links in the first email."
- "Tone: Be casual, human, and approachable. Avoid “salesy” language. Use softer CTAs (“worth a quick chat?”) instead of hard closes."
- "Personalization: Only use it if your variables are clean and accurate. Personalization can help a weak offer perform slightly better, but it won’t scale your campaign. What truly scales is a strong offer."

**Related rules (exact quotes).**
- Campaign setup: "Don’t track opens. Open data is unreliable and can hurt deliverability." / "Keep emails plain text — no fancy formatting." / "For SMB or mid-market campaigns, Saturdays often outperform weekdays."
- Lead-list formatting (affects copy variables): "Don’t use raw fields like {company-name} with “LLC” or “Inc.” included, or {title} with “Chief Executive Officer.” Keep names and titles natural for email copy."
- "Catch-Alls: If you use catch-all emails, put them in a separate campaign."
- Measuring: "the only reliable measure of deliverability is reply rate ... Send at least 1,000 emails and wait 48 hours before making any conclusions ... Updated Rule: Look at averages per client."
- Inbox placement tests: "Testing copy (e.g., does the version with spammy words perform worse?) ... Send with copy -> if spam -> send with adjusted copy till not spam (assuming domain is not fried)"
- 22k replies analysis: "77% of all replies were automated." / "Industry chatter says that super short, “non-salesy” copy combined with aged domains works best for Outlook. We haven’t validated that yet" / "Experiment with Outlook-specific copy ... test ultra-short, no-offer messaging and older domains"
- MVP outbound: "Send plain-text, short copy. Track replies only."
- Cold-traffic-ready offer: "You need a cold-traffic-ready offer — something that’s simple, obvious, and makes sense within 10 seconds." GOOD: "“We help B2B agencies get 10 qualified appointments per month, guaranteed” is cold-traffic ready." BAD: "“We help businesses with marketing automation and process optimization” is not."
- On clients rewriting copy: "they rewrite all the copy, change every line, add unnecessary complexity ... outbound doesn’t work that way. It’s not about perfect creative — it’s about consistent iteration."
- "Even with an average offer, you can win with volume."
- "Leads = Bad": "A lead is someone who shows direct interest ... If they reply, “Can you send me more info?” or “Yeah, what’s pricing look like?”—that’s a lead." (reply handling; then call, LinkedIn, 14-day nurture with proof every other day).
- "Cold email still works, but it’s getting harder every year ... diversify your channels."

**Examples:** only the two offer lines above; no full emails in the readable text.

## D56. A LEAD ACADEMY DOCUMENT: "The Introduction To Cold Email" and "Crafting Effective Subject Lines" (2024, Google Docs)

- **Links:** Intro https://docs.google.com/document/d/12_HMhJy9a2Vt_8uIpxvurDZRMx17La1xHfiCieoij9Y/edit (2024-08-26); Subject lines https://docs.google.com/document/d/1DJ_flOPVKrdjf4Y2erKTs-FIiKuCLBv6zEIrK0v1Y1I/edit (2024-08-22).

Alex's own updated version is D19 (Agency Ignition, Dec 2025), which adds "We also don’t track open rates" and his personal picks. Treat D19 as the Dulcy rule; this is the source text.

Having a good subject line will increase your open rate and the chance of people answering your emails ... without converting subject lines, everything else in the process will not work." (Note: Alex's D19 later downplays this: "This initial step is becoming less important year over year".)

Rule (verbatim): "Cold email works so well because of the hyper-targeting you can abuse with platforms like Apollo.io, Crunchbase and more. You can get access to funding, date of creation, headcount, location, and a lot of things that makes it seem personable". No examples.


## D57. Script Emails (Google Doc) — 3D product configurator campaigns

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1WXKVx-b0htwhGSGpiqSfRxtFG9hNdHjepSJH9WgN6Rg/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-01-02
- **Author / owner:** io@antoniofurioso.com (third party; an e-commerce 3D configurator provider, likely a 2024-25 prospect/client of Dulcy; status UNKNOWN). CLIENT-SPECIFIC case names (FormiTable/Bart, Checkered Design, Janina) must not be reused.

**Summary.** Three 4-step drafts (SPORTS; Test 1; Test 2 with spintax versions) plus a ChatGPT personalization prompt. Structure: 1) subject + question; 2) follow-up + lead magnet (video); 3) subject + testimonial; 4) "Hail Mary" or value step.

**Label.** Mixed. Good: short, one question CTA, video micro-yes. Bad by Dulcy rules: links/testimonial URL in step 3, "HAILY MARY" guilt line ("do you want your competitors take another slice of the cake?"), unverified "more than 40%"/"109%" claims.

**Prompt (verbatim) — ChatGPT prompt for personalization.**
> Using the provided store link, create a natural, human-like, 15-word personalization sentence for an email. It should be a compliment or a thoughtful observation about the store, its products, or user experience. Avoid spammy or overly promotional language. Keep the tone conversational and relatable. Write it in a 5th degree reading level. Do not sound like an AI. Use only the information that you find on the website. Give as a output only the personalized sentence without any other symbols or words outside of it.

**Examples (verbatim).**

SPORTS
- 1st email w/ subject line + question. subject line: configuration of the products
> Ciao {{first_name}}, you can increase your conversions by more than 40% just by allowing your customers to personalize {{company_name}} products.
> As soon as FormiTable implemented the configurator in its e-commerce they close 3 big deals.
> Can I send you a video where I show you how they did it?
> {{signature}}
- 2nd email follow-up + lead magnet
> {{first_name}}, Bart closed 3 deals since he implemented the product customizer for is custom table brand.
> Do you want to listen what Bart says?
> Ciao, {{signature}}
- 3rd email w/ subject line + question lead magnet. subject line: Janina’s customers personalize their own jersey
> {{first_name}}, your clients will abandon your eCom if you do not offer what they want.
> For example, Janina’s customers do not hesitate and convert as soon they finished to personalize the jersey.
> {{company_name}} can do the same and increase conversions more than 40%.
> Can I send you more information about it?
> {{signature}}
- 4th email + HAILY MARY
> {{company_name}} eCom experience can improve of 109% just with a simple feature.
> It means: better customer experience -> more conversions -> more profit margins.
> {{first_name}}, {{random | is this not an interest for you? | do you want your competitors take another slice of the cake?}}
> {Cheers|Best|Ciao}
> %signature%

Test 1 Script Email
- 1st. subject line: PRODUCT configuration
> Hi NAME,
> PERSONALIZATION15
> I helped Checkered Design reduce design processing time by 80% by implementing a configurator for their jersey for their Shopify store.
> Would you like to see how this could save hours and increase conversions?
- 2nd
> NAME, do you want to watch how I build a PRODUCT configurator?
> The video will teach you how to build on your own and increase your store conversions.
- 3rd. subject line: Janina loves the configurator
> {{first_name}} , your clients deserve a better customer experience.
> For example, Janina wanted to let her customers design their products online but found it time-consuming to handle requests manually.
> She saves 80% of her time with it. Look what Janina says about it:
> [How Janina and Douglas are revolutionizing the sport market](YouTube link)
> %signature%
- 4th email + value (reviews on YouTube/ doc of AR tools)
> GIF [STILL WAITING]
> Meanwhile, here are the tools I use to create 3D experiences for e-commerce:
> LINK
> Let me know if you’d like me to show you how these tools can work for your store.
> PS: these tools can help you create immersive shopping experiences that increase engagement and conversions.

Test 2 Script email (plain, then spintax version)
- 1st. subject line: configurator
> {Hi|Hey|Ciao} NAME, Checkered Design increased their product design customization by 80% by implementing a 3D configurator in their Shopify store.
> Would you like to see how this could make you reduce hours and increase conversions?
> %signature%
> -----
> {Hi|Hey|Hello|Ciao} NAME, {Checkered Design|a leading brand|our client, Checkered Design} {increased|boosted|enhanced} their product design customization by {80%|a remarkable 80%|over 80%} by implementing a {3D configurator|custom 3D tool|interactive configurator} in their Shopify store.
> {Would you like to see|Can I show you|Are you interested in seeing} how this could {help you save hours|reduce your workload|save time} and {increase|boost|improve} conversions?
> %signature%
- 2nd
> NAME, do you want to watch how to build a 3D configurator?
> %signature%
> PS: The video will teach you how to build on your own and increase your store conversions.
> -----
> {Hi|Hello|Hey|Ciao} {{first_name}}, {do you want to see how to | would you like to watch how to | are you interested in watching how to} {build|create|set up} a {3D configurator|custom 3D tool|product configurator}?
> %signature%
> PS: {The video will teach you|You'll learn from the video|This video shows you} how to {build it yourself|create one on your own|develop it independently} and {increase|boost|improve} your store conversions.
- 3rd. subject line: Janina loves the configurator
> NAME, your clients deserve a better customer experience.
> For example, Janina wanted to let her customers design their products online but found it time-consuming to handle requests manually.
> She saves 80% of her time with it. Look what Janina says about it:
> LINK TESTIMONIAL
> %signature%
> -----
> {Hi|Hello|Hey|Ciao} {{first_name}}, {your clients deserve|your customers deserve|it's time to offer your clients} a better customer experience.
> For example, {Janina|one of our clients, Janina|a business owner like you, Janina} {wanted|was looking|hoped} to let her customers {design|customize|personalize} their products online but found it {time-consuming|overwhelming|difficult} to handle requests manually.
> She {saved | cut} 80% of her time by {implementing | installing} the configurator. {Look what Janina says about it|Here's what Janina has to say|Check out Janina's experience}:

- 4th email + HAILY MARY

> NAME, if you are not interested in increasing this now, can I contact you in 2 months?

> NAME, {If you're not interested in increasing this now|If this isn't a priority for you at the moment|Should you want to revisit this later}, {can I contact you|would it be okay if I reached out|is it fine to follow up} in {2 months|a couple of months|60 days}?
> %signature%

## D58. Mastering Cold Email / "Mastering Personalized Email: A Comprehensive Guide to Unlocking Business Growth" (Google Doc)

- **Link:** https://docs.google.com/document/d/1yizDt_eYSjkheWUv6EA5W_JLdxvTXgfSbwrBmL1y4B8/edit
- **Last modified:** 2025-03-26
- **Author / owner:** d@trustscout.com (TrustScout; third party). ~146k characters; 0% overlap with export. Read in full; sections: Intro, Why Email Is Relevant Again, Use Cases for 1:1 Email, Infrastructure, Sending Tools, Leads, **Copy**, Compliance, Steps to Get Out of Spam, Key Tools, Final Checks, Put 5M Emails To Work, The Case for AI. Only the Copy section is copywriting; the rest is infra, tools, compliance and a product pitch. Focus is B2C real-estate 1:1 email (homeowner examples).

**Framework (verbatim, "5 S").** "1. Subject - Must be STRONG 2. Specific - Hyper-personalized 3. Situational - About THEM (Address their pain points / Provide value through social proof / Pique curiosity / Not boring / bad) 4. Simple - 6 lines or less 5. SPAM - avoid bad words & keep it CANSPAM compliant". Also PAS and AIDA with examples.

**Rules (exact quotes).**
- Hormozi: "“If you can describe the problem better than they can, they’ll assume you have the solution.”" and "“Make your offer so good, people feel stupid saying no.”"
- Aaron Ross: "Write as if you’re explaining your offer to a third grader—simple, direct, and easy to digest." / "Limit emails to 3-6 sentences." / "Focus on One Idea"
- "Avoid spammy language like “act now,” “free,” or “guaranteed.” Instead, write naturally and make sure to: Include a clear sender name. Add your physical address. Offer an easy way to unsubscribe."
- "No Spam Flags: Avoid links, videos, or overly promotional language."
- Checklist: "Is it under 50 characters to display fully on mobile?" (subject) / "Is the email under 6 lines total?" / "Is the language written at a 3rd–6th grade reading level with short sentences?" / "Are there 2–3 follow-up emails planned?"
- Bad/Good: "Bad: “We are excited to offer market-leading real estate valuation services.” Good: “Let’s figure out what your home is worth—it might surprise you.”"

**Sample cold email (verbatim; third-party B2C example; hard 15-min CTA = against Dulcy's soft-CTA rule).**
> Subject: Thinking of selling? Here’s what’s happening in [Neighborhood].
> Hi [First Name],
> I noticed your home in [Neighborhood] has been on the market for [X days]. Many homeowners in the area are facing similar challenges attracting the right buyers.
> Last month, I helped a family on [Street Name] sell their home for 12% over asking in just 10 days by using targeted marketing strategies. With homes in [Neighborhood] selling quickly, this could be a great time to maximize your investment.
> Would you be open to a quick 15-minute call next week to explore your options?
> Best, [Your Name] [Your Contact Information]

Follow-up framework (verbatim): "Email 1: Introduction ... “Hi [Name], I specialize in helping homeowners in [Neighborhood] navigate [specific challenge]. Let’s connect!” / Email 2: Social Proof ... “Hi [Name], I recently worked with [similar homeowner] and helped them achieve [specific result]. Would you be open to a chat?” / Email 3: Urgency ... “Hi [Name], the market in [Neighborhood] is shifting quickly—can we connect this week?”"


## D59. End To End Outbound Sales Manual - Master Document - Updated (.docx; also Google Doc and PDF)

- **Links:** .docx https://drive.google.com/file/d/19LFRKccwjwNT64l2_6JfFuZIl2Klyrp_/view (read, 2025-12-13 upload); Google Doc original https://docs.google.com/document/d/1b9Tib69SeXIaCzI6NzJ-PUPTEn93OcZnocCriXn8dIE/edit (owner adminsupport@closersintoleaders.com, 2022-02-16; same content); PDF 166RKRiw2v9S9R3-W5FFOYRMtpY1UCvTV.
- **Author:** Closers.io / "Closers into Leaders" (Jared Warwick). Third-party, 2021-22 (Mailshake era). Its Email Creative, Testing Guidelines and KPIs sections are the same text as D38/D39.

**Copy-relevant rules (exact quotes).**
- Personalization scale: "Understand - scale of personalization 1 → 10 / More personalized, higher response, less output / Less personalized, lower response, more output / Key is finding balance."
- "How to write good 1st lines: Tip off that it’s a personalized email (I saw you… / I heard you… / Compliment) / Ambiguity (is this a sales email? Or a potential client?) / The initial question"
- Proofreading: "Say it outloud / Run on sentences / Trying to say too much" and the "“Bar test”".
- First-line instruction: "Write a personalized first line (this will be the first line of the email the lead will receive). Alternate between Hi NAME, Hey NAME & NAME. The first sentence should be personalized to their specific business. The second sentence will always be the same - “Do you currently have a sales team that handles your client enrollment?”."
- Tips: "Keep it conversational ... Avoid sounding too formal or robotic." / "Avoid using the company’s name in the first line - The company name you have may not be current ... and you use the wrong one, they will immediately realize you don’t know anything about them." / "Say it out loud"
- "Have a backup first line so if the system can not find a first-line variable it doesnt leave it blank" (matches D1's blank-variable rule).
- "Set up follow-up emails and space 5 days apart" (old Closers.io timing; Alex's D25 says 1-2 days).
- Reporting: track "Successful first lines - Tracking which custom first lines perform better than others."

**First-line examples (verbatim; third-party; good per its own method).**
- "Hi Mitchell - Saw what you’re doing with gym owners on instagram and wanted to reach out personally. Do you currently have a sales team that handles your client enrollment?"
- "Hey Mitchell - Saw what you're doing with business owners in the health & wellness space and wanted to reach out personally. Do you currently have a sales team that handles your enrollment?"
- "Mitchell - Came across some of the consulting work you're doing for prospective college students on Facebook and wanted to reach out personally. Do you currently have a sales team that handles your enrollment?"
- "Hi Mitchell - Came across your soccer mentoring program on Facebook the other day and wanted to reach out personally. Do you currently have a sales team that handles your enrollment?"
- Fallback variable: "{{firstline:Hey - I was checking out your Facebook and noticed the work you do in the space and wanted to reach out personally and ask if you currently have a sales team that handles your client enrollment?}}"

**Reply example (verbatim; Sean's area):** “Hey NAME, thanks for the response. Good question and kinda depends on what you need help with right now/what our engagement would look like. I say that because we work with 8 fig companies down to companies doing 5k/m and hundreds in-between. So as you can imagine what we do for each company varies, as does the investment. I can definitely give you more context the more info I have on what you might be looking for and as opposed to going back and forth here writing novels, would you have a super quick 5 to connect today? I could make some time at XYZ or XYZ. Let me know. Jared” (Note: D7 says don't thank the prospect or say "good question".)

The rest (team roles, lead databases Seamless/ZoomInfo/Cognism/Lusha, Neverbounce/Clearoutphone cleaning, Mailshake setup, Slack/HubSpot sync) is list-building and tech, not copy.


## D60. LuckyDiem copy: "2025 Sales Sequence v1.0" and "Copy of LuckyDiem's Scripts" (Google Docs)

- **Links:** 2025 Sales Sequence v1.0 https://docs.google.com/document/d/1kCvhfUeEosf1rPYxFHyCoi0RIPzaInEMtCLMfuy3ZTU/edit (owner andrew@luckydiem.com, modified 2025-10-01; the client's own sequence); Copy of LuckyDiem's Scripts https://docs.google.com/document/d/1q2vKkLmaUFga-e4pUj0c1clnHBJqUwEKJcsIRl6rHtE/edit (owner andrew@luckydiem.com, modified 2025-11-17; Dulcy campaign copy with dated revisions: "New Copy October 27, 2025 / Revised Nov 17, 2025", "NEW VERSION Oct 8, 2025").
- **Client:** LuckyDiem (card-linked offers, CLO; senders Andrew, CEO & Founder, and Serene Chan, Head of Marketing). Past client, not one of the current six. CLIENT-SPECIFIC claims (200M/400M/500M cardholders, Nike/Disney+/Food Lion/Motel 6 20–50x ROAS, 30–40% order value) must never be reused.

**Most useful item — labelled best performer (verbatim, from the client's doc).** "This email got a quick open and response:"
> Hi Jeff,
> How is the CDLX campaign going? I am curious to see how the new multi-tier offers playout. What do the analytics indicate on offering the consumer 15% cash back? Is this for new only? Otherwise, in our experience the ROI could be stronger with other types of constructs.
> How's your calendar looking for a call next week?
> Thanks,
(Pattern: knowledge-as-proof question about the prospect's live campaign with a competitor; matches the "investment piece" method in D7/D16. Dulcy then templated it as the Oct 8, 2025 Email 1 below.)

**Dulcy's Oct 8, 2025 "NEW VERSION" (verbatim; "MAKE SURE THESE EMAILS COME FROM SERENE@luckydiem.co").**
- Email 1. Subject: Quick thought on Cardlytics ROI
> Hi {FIRST_NAME},
> Curious — are you seeing any meaningful lift from your {cash back rate} Cardlytics campaign, or is that just for new customers? In our experience, ROI often climbs faster with other offer constructs.
> Worth a quick chat next week to compare notes?
> Best, Serene Chan / Head of Marketing / LuckyDiem
- Email 2. Subject: A Smarter way to CLO
> Hi {FIRST_NAME},
> Since you’re already running CLO with Cardlytics, you know the channel works. However, what we’re seeing is brands want better economics + unified data across multiple partners—and that’s exactly where LuckyDiem is outperforming.
> Would you be open to a 15-min call to see how we’re helping brands like {COMPANY} get more from CLO?
- Email 3. Subject: Nike → 50x ROAS from CLO
> Hi {FIRST_NAME},
> Brands like Nike, Disney+, Food Lion, and Motel 6 are seeing 20–50x ROAS from LuckyDiem-powered CLO campaigns. The reason: we’re not just another publisher—we’re a full network with over 500M connect cards with one contract, no channel conflict, and scale across all major platforms.
> Would you like me to share our deck to learn more?
- Email 4. Subject: Why CEOs and CFOs love CLO
> Hi {FIRST_NAME},
> CLO is winning C-suites over because it protects full-price sales—cashback is booked as marketing spend, not a discount. That means cleaner P&L, predictable ROI, and happier investors.
> Should I send you a quick breakdown of how brands are framing CLO to finance teams?
- Email 5. Subject: Fill CLO gaps Cardlytics can’t
> Hi {FIRST_NAME},
> LuckyDiem has a network of 500M+ linked cards. If you’re aiming for more efficiency + visibility in CLO, we are happy to send over a deck for you to learn more.

**CDLX campaign (OLD) variants (verbatim).**
1. "Hi {FIRST_NAME}, I saw {COMPANY} is running {CASHBACK%} offer on global card networks. Is that rate set just for new customers, or across the board? I ask because I’ve seen some brands shift their CLOs to a pay-per-sale setup (instead of clicks or impressions) and end up with 30–40% higher order values and 20–30% more repeat visits. Want me to mock up how that might look for {COMPANY}?"
2. "Hi {FIRST_NAME}, I saw {COMPANY} is running {CASHBACK%} on global card networks. If that’s across the board, there’s usually 30–40% more revenue left on the table — and it only takes 10 minutes to test. You’d only pay when sales come in. Want me to mock up how we’d capture that for {COMPANY}?"
3. "Hi {FIRST_NAME}, I saw {COMPANY} running {CASHBACK%} on global card networks. Most CLOs work, but what we hear a lot is frustration around transparency and scattered reporting. We set it up so brands see everything in one place and only pay when sales come in. Want me to mock up what that would look like for {COMPANY}?"
4. "Hi {FIRST_NAME}, I saw {COMPANY} has {CASHBACK%} live on global card networks. Are you managing that across different partners? - "AL version for DTC (OLD)" = the same A–G expanded with "LuckyDiem is the only company integrated across ..." claims (read; not repeated).

**DTC 11-17-25 (Oct 27, revised Nov 17, 2025; verbatim subjects + bodies, signed "— Serene", each with the same P.S. "LuckyDiem is the only company that is integrated across major financial institutions (Bank of America, Citi, Mastercard, Amex, etc.) and affiliate platforms (CJ, Impact, Rakuten, etc.) taking performance marketing to a new level of reach and ease.").**
- Email 1 “DTC growth without the Meta tax”: "Hi [name], Customer acquisition costs aren’t just high — they’re unsustainable. Retail Brew’s recent piece on “What’s Next for DTC” nailed it: the platforms that once powered growth have become the new middlemen. LuckyDiem helps DTC brands acquire customers through Card-Linked Offers — ads delivered inside bank and fintech apps where consumers already spend. You only pay on verified sales, not clicks or impressions. Our partners are seeing 40-70% lower CAC compared to Meta or Google. Worth a quick chat to see if it fits your mix?"
- Email 2 “First-party data. Zero cookies.”: "Hi [name], As cookies disappear and iOS 14 limits tracking, LuckyDiem gives brands bank-verified, first-party transaction data — all privacy-compliant. You can target by spend category, measure real incremental lift, and retarget buyers without any pixels or IDs. CLOs let you reach real customers — not anonymous clicks. Want me to show you how we’ve used this for other DTC brands?"
- Email 3 “Turn purchases into your new performance channel”: "Hi [name], Most DTC growth playbooks are built on ads that stop at the click. LuckyDiem connects all the way through to the purchase, both online and in-store. We partner with banks and apps reaching 500M+ cardholders, driving measurable ROAS and transparent attribution — no guesswork, no cookies, no wasted spend. Would you be open to a 15-minute intro to see how this could drop your CAC and boost repeat sales?"
(Email 1 uses a third-party authority hook, "Retail Brew", matching D13's rule that authority must be one the buyer trusts.)

**Client's own 2025 Sales Sequence (summary; Andrew's copy, pre-Dulcy, longer and harder CTAs).** Email 1 “A smarter way to run CLO campaigns?” (bulleted benefits + calendar link); Email 2 “How Food Lion, Motel 6 and Nike are driving 10–50x ROAS with CLO” ("Just wanted to follow up in case you missed this" opener, 15-min ask); Email 3 “C-Suite Loves CLO” (long finance explanation); Final “Worth a 15-min test drive?” ("just reply with “info.”" CTA); LinkedIn connection/DM/follow-up; re-engagement email “Picking this back up—CLO still on your radar?” + follow-ups “Just circling back—still exploring CLO?” and “Close the loop?”. Label: client baseline; the "Close the loop?" and "Just circling back" lines are the lazy/breakup patterns Dulcy avoids (D7, D25).

# Part 4. Client-specific facts found (NEVER reuse one client's facts, proof or numbers for another client or for Dulcy)

Each block below belongs to one client only. Facts marked client-written or unverified must be confirmed with that client before use. Current clients per CLAUDE.md: 6 plus Dulcy; the names searched were Twenty West Media, Lead FLO Marketing, Human Marketing, The Marketing Cartel, MJJ Brilliant, Global Media Group.

### Lead FLO Marketing (source D1, D4)
- Client contact: Patrick LeMaster; EmailBison workspace 68 (per D1).
- Services per onboarding and leadflo.marketing: paid advertising, SEO, CRM/follow-up, website creation and lead-generation landing pages.
- Verticals in play (Oct 2026): Tree services/arborists (started Southern California), HVAC (nationwide), Event Rentals (nationwide per Aug 21 call; reconfirm).
- Offer contracts: PAGE-1 = free page draft + editable text + recorded walkthrough, publishing separate (PROPOSED, not approved). LEADS-5 = five free rental inquiries for Event Rentals (agreed Aug 21 as a direction; current supply and areas unconfirmed).

- The "12 companies" claim is unsupported and was removed. No Tree/HVAC/Event numerical case study exists. Patrick's August Event figures were verbal and approximate; not usable in copy.

### MJJ Brilliant (source D2, D3)
- Client contact: Ike.
- "family-owned and operated jewelry manufacturer since 1982" (client-supplied background line).
- Wholesale repairs: wholesale pricing; "turnaround time of 10 business days on agreed jobs" (qualifier required).
- Manufacturing: "no-MOQ made-to-order options, 0% duty on qualifying USMCA production and 2–3-week delivery lead times, depending on the piece and order" (all qualifiers required).
- Team "checks in with production and keeps you updated" (supported by an archived Oct 13 call).
- Banned for MJJ: free repairs, price-beating claim, fake breakup lines, false prior relationship.

- (D4) Lead FLO pricing example from onboarding: $1,000 one-time setup fee plus prepaid lead wallet, e.g. $2,000 for 20 qualified leads at $100 each; must be set by vertical. Priority verticals: roofing, tree service, plumbing, HVAC, kitchen and bath remodeling, general contracting, landscaping. Channels: Google Ads, Meta, TikTok. Differentiator: exclusive opportunities pre-vetted against client-specific criteria (vs Angi/Thumbtack/HomeAdvisor shared leads). Booked appointments and deposit collection are future capabilities, not promises. Unverified tree-service story: ~$4,000 invested, ~$30k to ~$60k monthly revenue in 60 days (do not use publicly until Patrick verifies). Exclusion URLs requested: Wellbiz Brands, Drybar, Amazing Lash Studio.
